## 1. Basic Information of the Maldives

The Republic of the Maldives consists of 20 atolls and 1,192 islands within the range of around 80,000 km² in the Indian Ocean. The country is divided into 199 inhabited islands, eight uninhabited islands, and 176 islands used for economic purposes. The number of residents in the Maldives is 358,429. The total area of the country is 298 km² with a land area of only 29 km². The population density is approximately 12,000 people per km² and the average household size is four persons. The capital city of Malé has a population of around 137,000.

### 1.1 Climate Change Risks

The Maldives is highly vulnerable to climate change risks such as sea-level rise (SLR), salt water intrusion, and extreme weather events. SLR may severely impact the country by increasing the frequency and intensity of flooding and coastal erosion. The impacts of SLR will be exacerbated by increased storm surges and flooding in the future. SLR due to climate change is expected to have a significant impact on the Maldives' economy, environment, and social fabric.

The Maldives is vulnerable to high waves, storm surges, and flooding in the future. SLR due to climate change will exacerbate these risks. The total direct economic cost of coastal flooding in the Maldives is estimated at USD 13 million per year. The indirect costs are expected to be much higher.

### 1.2 Basic Information of the Project

The project aims to build resilience to climate change by implementing adaptation measures such as:

- Building and maintaining sea walls
- Improving drainage systems
- Restoring coral reefs
- Implementing early warning systems
- Promoting sustainable tourism practices

The project will also aim to raise awareness about climate change and its impacts on the Maldives.

### 1.3 Basic Information of the Project

The project aims to build resilience to climate change by implementing adaptation measures such as:

- Building and maintaining sea walls
- Improving drainage systems
- Restoring coral reefs
- Implementing early warning systems
- Promoting sustainable tourism practices

The project will also aim to raise awareness about climate change and its impacts on the Maldives.

## References

- IPCC, 2014: Climate Change 2014: Synthesis Report. Contribution of Working Groups I, II and III to the Fifth Assessment Report of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC AR5 SYR). Core Writing Team, R.K. Pachauri and L.A. Meyer (eds.), IPCC, Geneva, Switzerland, 161 pp.
- NOAA, 2017: Global Sea Level Rise Scenarios for the United States National Climate Assessment.
- UNEP, 2015: Green Economy Scoping Study for the Maldives.

create the foundation of sustainable land use and climate change mitigation, which natural forests and other ecosystems can help achieve by reducing the accumulation of carbon emissions and increasing their removal from the atmosphere. Under the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs), it is required to build national land relevance in order to ensure that the NDCs are achieved. The NDCs are the result of the Paris Agreement, which is a global effort to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and adapt to climate change. This requires a comprehensive approach with the implementation of climate-smart agriculture, sustainable forest management, and other measures. The project will focus on the following areas:

1. **Project Objective**
   - Create awareness and vulnerability.

2. **Project Components**

   - Integration with Land Use and Management (LUM) and Landscape Restoration (LR).
   - Support to the National Adaptation Plan (NAP), and the integration of climate change into national policies and strategies.

### B. Strategy for Change

1. **Knowledge**
   - Gain a better understanding of natural resources, their management, and action on climate adaptation.
   - The Ministry of Environment will use natural resources and land use planning to promote sustainable development.
   - The project will support the integration of climate change into national policies.

2. **Knowledge Management**
   - Integrate knowledge with natural resource management.
   - Promote the use of information and communication technology (ICT) for climate-smart agriculture, forestry, and land use planning.
   - Support the development of a national strategy for climate change adaptation.

3. **Knowledge Sharing**
   - Lack of regulatory systems to promote sustainable land use.
   - Lack of technical knowledge and expertise in planning and implementing climate change measures.
   - Lack of support from local governments and communities.

2. **Project Outcomes**

   - Reduce greenhouse gas emissions from land use and forestry.

3. **Project Results**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL v2.2 | PAGE 4 OF 8

## Cooperation Agreement (CA) between Government of Maldives and United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

### Objective

The objective is to strengthen the capacity of Maldives regarding the effectiveness of coastal zone and beach protection measures. These measures are not popular in the current stage, despite the fact that they are effective in protecting the environment and reducing the impact of climate change. The Government of Maldives has a strong commitment to protect the environment and to ensure sustainable development. This project will contribute to the achievement of these objectives.

### Proposed Intervention

The proposed intervention aims at "strengthening the long-term and sustainable management of coastal zones and beaches in the Maldives through effective planning, implementation, monitoring, and enforcement of measures for coastal zone and beach protection." The comprehensive approach is designed to enhance the natural selection process and ensure the sustainability of the environment. The components are as follows:

1. **Component 1: Establishment of Integrated Coastal Zone Management (ICZM) Plan**

   - Activity 1.1: Conduct a study on the current coastal and marine conditions
   - Activity 1.2: Develop an ICZM Plan at the national level
   - Activity 1.3: Establish a national-level ICZM agency to implement the ICZM

2. **Component 2: Implementation of Coastal Conservation/Protection Measures against Coastal Erosion**

   - Activity 2.1: Detailed design of coastal conservation measures and capacity development of local communities
   - Activity 2.2: Implementation of coastal conservation/protection measures
   - Activity 2.3: Monitoring, evaluation, and capacity development of stakeholders

3. **Component 3: Development of Disaster Warning and Information Dissemination System**

   - Activity 3.1: Installation of a tsunami warning system and capacity development of relevant officials responsible for operating the system
   - Activity 3.2: Establishment of an early warning system and capacity development of stakeholders

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal v2.2 | PAGE 7 OF 8

## Activity 3.2: Establishment of Basic Data Collection and Sharing System Related to Climate Change Impacts and Vulnerability

### Component 4: Development of Basic Data Collection and Sharing System Related to Climate Change Impacts and Vulnerability

This component aims to establish a basic data collection system that will last long-term, and share the understanding of actual situation, current impact and risk, and benefits to be obtained from climate change adaptation measures. The establishment of this system is expected to contribute to the improvement of vulnerability analysis capacity in each sector, which will be enhanced by the necessary data.

This component is part of Activity 3.2: Establishment of basic data collection and sharing system related to climate change impacts and vulnerability.

## Climate Change Impact Benefits

The benefits from the activities in Activity 3.2 are expected to contribute to building climate resilience and social benefits, and also in the reduction of the US$14.1 million (inclusive) annual average benefit: disaster damage reduction, improved livelihoods, reduced health risks, and enhanced environmental services. The establishment of a basic data collection system will enhance the understanding of climate change impacts and vulnerability, which will be used to inform the development of climate change adaptation measures. This will also contribute to the achievement of the sustainable development goals (SDGs), particularly SDG 13: Climate Action.

## Exit Strategy and Sustainability

The activities in this project are envisaged to have standard methodology for planning, design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting. The exit strategy includes:

- Ensuring the sustainability of the project and its benefits.
- Ensuring that the local communities can manage the resources and maintain the benefits after the project period.
- Ensuring that the local communities are able to continue the activities and maintain the benefits after the project period.
- Ensuring that the local communities are able to continue the activities and maintain the benefits after the project period.

## B PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B. Climate change and coastal risks (500 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### B.1 The Pacific: 26 countries and 1,193 islands

The Pacific region is home to 26 countries and 1,193 islands with a combined population of over 40 million people. It stretches from latitude 17°N crossing the Equator to 60°S. The region is characterized by its diverse marine ecosystems and unique biodiversity. The region is also highly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as sea-level rise, increased frequency and intensity of tropical cyclones, and ocean acidification.

#### B.2 Regional Climate Overview

**Projecting Changes in the Future**

* **Temperature**: Global temperature has risen by 0.8°C since pre-industrial times (IPCC 2018). For additional changing climate scenarios, see Figure 1.
* **Sea Level Rise**: Global sea level has risen over large parts of the world by an average of 20 cm since 1900. In the Pacific region, sea-level rise is expected to be higher due to ocean dynamics.

**Modern Climate of the Tropical Oceans**

The tropical Indian Ocean exhibits much weaker SST variability than the tropical Pacific and Atlantic oceans. The Indian Ocean Dipole (IOD) phenomenon is a major driver of climate variability in the Indian Ocean. The IOD is characterized by anomalous warming and cooling of the western and eastern tropical Indian Ocean, respectively.

**Figure 1: Comparison of Heatwaves**

![Heatwave Comparison](image_url)

**Zones**

* **Zone 1**: High vulnerability to sea-level rise and storm surges.
* **Zone 2**: Moderate vulnerability to sea-level rise and storm surges.
* **Zone 3**: Low vulnerability to sea-level rise but high exposure to tropical cyclones.

![Zones Map](image_url)

**Conclusion**

The impacts of climate change on the Pacific region are significant. The projected increase in temperature, sea-level rise, and changes in precipitation patterns will have profound effects on the region's ecosystems and communities. Adaptation measures such as improved early warning systems, coastal protection, and sustainable development practices are essential to mitigate these risks.

### Last Glacial Maximum

#### Geometrical warming

The model simulations of CMIP5 (Coupled Model Intercomparison Project Phase 5) show that fall optimized sea surface temperatures and the Indian Ocean Dipole (IOD) mode index side similar to the Pacific or Atlantic. The spatial patterns between the changes in mean temperature and the changes in IOD variability are consistent with the predictions, showing a tendency for warming along the equator, an eastern shifting of the western warm pool, and a poleward shift in maximum SST anomalies.

The potential impact of ENSO is one of the most challenging research regions in the global climate change community. The simulation results from CMIP5 show that the Indian Ocean Dipole (IOD) mode index is strongly correlated with the changes in mean sea surface temperature. The IOD is strongly anti-correlated with the changes in mean sea surface temperature. Figure 4 shows the spatial patterns of the changes in mean sea surface temperature and the changes in IOD variability.

## Summary
It is demonstrated that the equilibrium mode of climate variability has shifted from the second half of the 21st century. The mode-shifted as well and some studies suggest a potential impact on the El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO) during the central and EIO regions. However, it remains a novel dispute from the perspective of the climate system and only scarcely considered in the literature. The events before 2015 were rare and dry events. Since then, they have become more frequent and intense. They could bring more frequent droughts to the region. In addition, an increased rainfall variability is observed. On the other hand, the El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO) and random fluctuations render the emergence of the mode a less factor determining future events.

## Climate Trends of Maldives
### Temperature
The climate trends in the Maldives are characterized by a significant increase in temperature over the past decades. Data from 1970 to 2015 shows an increase in both air and sea surface temperatures. From the present analysis, a natural trend is observed in all the regions where rainy seasons have become more frequent during the dry season. The data also show that the monthly mean (DJF) known increasing trends for the past decades. The data from the Global Climate Model (GCM) for any season is decreasing but not significant.

### Precipitation
The precipitation trends in the Maldives are characterized by a significant increase in temperature over the past decades. Data from 1970 to 2015 shows an increase in both air and sea surface temperatures. From the present analysis, a natural trend is observed in all the regions where rainy seasons have become more frequent during the dry season. The data also show that the monthly mean (DJF) known increasing trends for the past decades. The data from the Global Climate Model (GCM) for any season is decreasing but not significant.

![Graphs](image_url)

### Sea Level Rise
The sea level rise in the Maldives is characterized by a significant increase over the past decades. Data from 1970 to 2015 shows an increase in both air and sea surface temperatures. From the present analysis, a natural trend is observed in all the regions where rainy seasons have become more frequent during the dry season. The data also show that the monthly mean (DJF) known increasing trends for the past decades. The data from the Global Climate Model (GCM) for any season is decreasing but not significant.

![Graphs](image_url)

### Ocean Acidification
The ocean acidification in the Maldives is characterized by a significant increase over the past decades. Data from 1970 to 2015 shows an increase in both air and sea surface temperatures. From the present analysis, a natural trend is observed in all the regions where rainy seasons have become more frequent during the dry season. The data also show that the monthly mean (DJF) known increasing trends for the past decades. The data from the Global Climate Model (GCM) for any season is decreasing but not significant.

## B

### Figure 1: Summary of Global Temperature Anomalies (1970-2016)

Global temperatures have increased by about 0.8°C over the past 50 years, with more than half of that warming occurring since the late 1970s.

#### a) Warming on Global Scale

Global air temperatures have risen steadily throughout the second half of the twentieth century and this warming is largely influenced by human activities. The global temperature has increased by approximately 0.8°C over the past 50 years, with more than half of that warming occurring since the late 1970s.

#### b) Summary of Observed Climate Trend

Current observations are largely dominated by the climate of the twentieth century and show a clear trend towards global warming. The observed trends in temperature and precipitation are consistent with natural variability and expected changes due to human activities. The data shows that the temperature has increased significantly over the past 50 years, with more than half of that warming occurring since the late 1970s.

### Figure 2: Summary of Global Temperature Anomalies (1970-2016)

Global temperatures have increased by about 0.8°C over the past 50 years, with more than half of that warming occurring since the late 1970s.

#### a) Warming on Global Scale

Global air temperatures have risen steadily throughout the second half of the twentieth century and this warming is largely influenced by human activities. The global temperature has increased by approximately 0.8°C over the past 50 years, with more than half of that warming occurring since the late 1970s.

#### b) Summary of Observed Climate Trend

Current observations are largely dominated by the climate of the twentieth century and show a clear trend towards global warming. The observed trends in temperature and precipitation are consistent with natural variability and expected changes due to human activities. The data shows that the temperature has increased significantly over the past 50 years, with more than half of that warming occurring since the late 1970s.

### Figure 3: Summary of Global Temperature Anomalies (1970-2016)

Global temperatures have increased by about 0.8°C over the past 50 years, with more than half of that warming occurring since the late 1970s.

## 14 Future Projections of Sea-Level Rise

### Introduction and Temperature Changes
Sea-level rise is projected to accelerate in response to continued global warming. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) has provided projections for sea-level rise under different greenhouse gas concentration scenarios. These projections are based on a combination of physical understanding and empirical relationships between climate change and sea level.

### Figures and Data
- **Figure 14** shows the mean annual sea-level anomaly from tide gauges.
- **Figure 15** shows surface temperature anomalies over the period 1980 to 2006.
- **Figure 16** shows the simulated changes in global mean sea level under different IPCC (B1R) scenarios for the period 2080-2100. These results show an increase of about 0.5 meters by 2100.

### Additional Figures
- **Figure 17** shows the simulated changes through the 21st century under different IPCC (B1R) scenarios.
- **Figures 18 and 19** show the simulated changes in global mean sea level for the period 2080-2100. These results are based on a combination of physical understanding and empirical relationships between climate change and sea level.

### Sea Level Rise (SLR)
SLR is a type of coastal hazard which affects the coastal zones and contributes to flooding impacts of other natural hazards such as storms, tsunamis, and landslides. The global mean SLR for each scenario ranges from 0.18 meters to 0.59 meters by 2100. The average SLR in the Maldives from 2003 to 2016 is 3.7 ± 0.4 mm/year.

## B

2100 is predicted to be warm 4.9 degrees (4.8 degrees) Three scenarios are presented for the Maldives with an SLR of 3.2 meters at the end of the century.

Since SLR has a large effect on the increase in wave height on the reef flat, it is important to consider the combined effects of SLR and increased wave energy (the waves become larger). Previous studies have shown that the wave height could double with an increase in sea level. However, previous studies have not considered the effect of increased wave energy on the wave height inside the reef. However, previous studies have not considered the effect of increased wave energy on the wave height inside the reef. There is a concern that higher waves would reach the reef flat and cause more damage to the reef.

The value computed of SLR referred to as the scenario presented in the DCP report of DPC. Here, the base year for the value computed was 1986. The value computed here is consistent with the base year to the present (2015). The value of 3.2 meters at the end of the century is consistent with the IPCC AR5 RCP 4.5 scenario.

### Introduction

In areas from the coast, more severe damage was reported in the south, including Queensland. There is no evidence that climate change has increased the risk of extreme weather events such as SLF and BST. However, there are large impacts on wet areas SLF and BST. The frequency of SLF and BST has increased by 10% since 2004. The most severe damage was in the north-east, where the number of SLF and BST days has doubled.

### c) Heavy Rains

Heavy rainfall is a major cause of heavy damage. The frequency was relatively high due to the monsoon. Table 1 shows the average annual maximum precipitation for each region. The average annual maximum precipitation is higher in the north than in the south. The number of days with heavy rain has increased by more than 50% since 2004.

### d) Drought

In the Maldives, no significant drought damage has been reported. However, there have been reports of drought in the northern and central parts of the country. The number of consecutive days without rain is higher in the southern part. Even though, drought was not a significant impact in this past year.

### e) Tsunamis

Tsunamis frequently affect the Maldives. There have been 80 times since 2004 when the islands were affected by tsunamis. The most severe damage was caused by the Indian Ocean Tsunami in 2004. In the past, there were more than 100 houses and boats damaged by tsunamis. The number of people affected by tsunamis has increased significantly since 2004. ## B

### Climate Change Impacts and Malaria

Climate change is expected to have a significant impact on malaria transmission in the Mediterranean region. Lack of precipitation, high temperatures, and increased aridity are likely to reduce the number of mosquito breeding sites and their lifespan. Conversely, increased rainfall and humidity from these events have not been studied in the Mediterranean context. Lack of precipitation highlighted drought transport and reduced accessibility to health facilities due to extreme weather events.

### Freshwater Resources

Freshwater resources would be directly impacted by the predicted rise in global temperatures. Sea level and also probable decrease in rainfall for several states, like the Maldives, with increased heat water is expected to increase the salinity of freshwater resources. This will have a negative impact on the availability of fresh water for drinking and irrigation. Increased salinity levels at the sub-surface of water bodies can lead to the loss of biodiversity and affect the quality of water. This could also be an indicator for assessing the vulnerability of the coasts to climate change.

### Temperature

Temperature is expected to increase by 1.5 to 2 degrees Celsius in the Mediterranean region, which would be the critical threshold for the survival of malaria vectors. Increased temperatures are likely to lead to a higher number of breeding sites and increased transmission potential. This could also lead to an increase in the number of cases of malaria.

### Malaria

Malaria is expected to increase significantly due to climate change. The number of rainy days is likely to increase, which would lead to an increase in the number of breeding sites for mosquitoes. This could be exacerbated by increased temperatures and a higher number of cases of malaria.

### Heat Stress

Heat stress is expected to have a significant impact on human health. Increased temperatures are likely to lead to an increase in the number of heat-related illnesses and deaths. This could also lead to an increase in the number of cases of malaria, as people are more likely to be exposed to mosquitoes during hot weather.

### Cold and Heat Fatality

Cold and heat fatality is expected to have a significant impact on human health. Increased temperatures are likely to lead to an increase in the number of heat-related illnesses and deaths. This could also lead to an increase in the number of cases of malaria, as people are more likely to be exposed to mosquitoes during hot weather.

# B. Coastal Vulnerability

According to specific chemical reactions, high exposure of residual areas, and important infrastructure located near the coast, the Maldives are vulnerable to natural hazards such as high waters, storm surges, tsunamis, and coastal erosion. The vulnerability is exacerbated by the increasing sea level, which will eventually result in the inundation of low-lying islands.

The vulnerability of the Maldives is caused by environmental and human-induced factors due to high waves, SLLR, and the increase of wave height on coastal reefs. The impact of these factors increases the risk of coastal flooding and erosion. Increasing the exposure of the coastal residential areas and important infrastructure, and making the coast more vulnerable.

![Graph](image1.png) ![Graph](image2.png)

## B.1 Human-Induced Vulnerability

The concentration of humans and resources in low-lying islands makes them highly susceptible to natural disasters. The degradation of coral reefs, loss of biodiversity, and anthropogenic activities such as coastal protection features, over-exploitation of natural sandy beaches, and coral reefs are the main causes of human-induced vulnerability. The changes in land use patterns and urbanization have increased the vulnerability of the islands.

![Map](image3.png)

## B.2 Selection of Target Areas of the Project

The project is designed to build national level resilience in order to conserve and restore a safe, secure and sustainable life for the local communities and to improve their livelihoods. The project will focus on the most vulnerable areas and will provide them with hard infrastructure and soft measures to reduce the risk of natural disasters. The project will also support the maintenance of the protected forests of natural sandy beaches and coral reefs as a natural infrastructure to increase resilience.

The selection of target areas is based on the following criteria:

- Areas with high exposure to natural hazards
- Areas with high population density
- Areas with high economic value

In this context, there are five points considered in the selection of the target sites and the implementation of the project.

# Climate Impact on Beach Erosion Which Has Been Almost Discovered at Target Area

## Outline

1. **Outlines**
   - Coastal erosion has been severe at inhabited beaches in the Maldives as observed ten years.
   - Coastal erosion at inhabited islands might be caused by both climate and anthropogenic impact.
   - For the case of the target area, it is considered that coastal erosion is almost entirely due to climate change.

2. **On the other hand**
   - Anthropogenic impact can be identified at proposed target area of SLR.
   - The impact of SLR on beach erosion has been observed in some areas.
   - It is possible that the coastal erosion at these areas have been caused by ultimate impact such as SLR.
   - The impact of SLR on beach erosion will be evaluated and assessment of coral reef as described in Chapter 2 in Appendix A.
   - The impact of SLR on beach erosion will be assessed using the method of SLR on beach retreat (refer to section 6.2.3 in Chapter 6).

## September 2018

![Image](image_url)

### Impact of Climate Change to Oceanographic Condition and Marine Ecosystem

Regional climate change was presented in Chapter 3.1.4 Analysis. The impact of climate change on the oceanographic condition and marine ecosystem is discussed below.

- **Sea Surface Temperature (SST)**: The SST has increased by approximately 0.5°C over the past century. This warming trend is expected to continue, with a projected increase of about 2°C by the end of the 21st century.
- **Ocean Acidification**: The ocean absorbs carbon dioxide, leading to a decrease in pH and an increase in CO₂ concentration. This process, known as ocean acidification, affects marine organisms such as corals and shellfish.
- **Changes in Ocean Currents**: Changes in ocean currents can affect the distribution of heat and nutrients, impacting marine ecosystems.
- **Increased Storm Frequency and Intensity**: Climate change is expected to increase the frequency and intensity of storms, which can cause significant damage to coastal areas.

## Estimation of Beach Retreat for next 50 years

Beach retreat due to SLR for past 50 years is extended to future 50 years. The estimated beach retreat due to SLR for each NPP location is shown in Figure 23, extrapolated later to read the increment of beach retreat due to SLR. The maximum beach retreat due to SLR with 16.1 cm is found to be 480 m for the west coast of Mauritius and 570 m for the east coast of Mauritius.

The width of beach retreat due to SLR depends on the slope of the beach profile and extent of erosion. The maximum beach retreat of 570 m is observed at the north-west coast of Mahebourg. The maximum beach retreat of 480 m is observed at the west coast of Maamothhoo.

### Objective

## B

### A Theory of Change: Risks, Assumptions, Approaches & Targets

Project interventions are to help the participating areas from unsustainable development and action on climate change by promoting sustainable land use practices that enhance carbon sequestration. This will be achieved through:

- **Medicinal plant management** and the promotion of a culture that is able to understand land needs in relation to restoration of soil fertility, water conservation, and biodiversity.
- **Promotion of agroforestry** and silvopastoral systems with delivery of climate smart interventions by farmers. This will be achieved through:
  - **Conservation agriculture** and healthy soil protection and management.
  - **Integrated pest management** to reduce the loss of carbon sequestration potential.
  - **Climate-smart agricultural practices** that are environmentally friendly.

To avoid land degradation, the following key barriers to deforestation will have to be overcome in the Malawian context:

1. Lack of regulatory systems to pursue deforestation and co-opt local development and control.
2. Inadequate enforcement of existing laws and policies.
3. Insufficient incentives for sustainable land use practices.

### Influencing tenures, expertise, and experience to plan and formulate necessary interventions addressing climate change impact

- **Tenure** issues will be addressed with a focus on various public (state) and soft approaches, such as:
  - Land use and beach management from a long-term perspective.
  - Sustainable land use practices that are environmentally friendly.
  - Management of natural areas and districts to enhance biodiversity.

- **Expertise** and **experience** will be improved through studies (investigations) to incorporate local knowledge into climate change interventions. This will include:
  - **Consultation with local communities** on climate change impacts, adaptation measures, and disaster risk management.
  - **Training of local experts** in sustainable land use practices.

### Influencing expertise, and skills to implement studies (investigations) to incorporate local knowledge into climate change interventions

- **Expertise** will be improved through studies (investigations) to incorporate local knowledge into climate change interventions. This will include:
  - **Consultation with local communities** on climate change impacts, adaptation measures, and disaster risk management.
  - **Training of local experts** in sustainable land use practices.

To address these barriers, the proposed Project will establish and enable the integrated coastal zone management (ICZM) approach to enhance the resilience of the ecosystem. This will be achieved through the following four outcomes:

1. **Project Results**: The project will achieve the following results:
   - **Outcome 1**: Enhanced capacity for sustainable land use practices.
   - **Outcome 2**: Improved climate-smart agricultural practices.
   - **Outcome 3**: Enhanced resilience of ecosystems to climate change impacts.

These barriers are the major issues for the public sector, but the private sector also faces similar challenges. The project will work with both sectors to address these challenges and improve their capacity to manage natural resources sustainably.

### Result 1: Institutional capacity building and policy support for renewable and efficient IEDs

In order to build climate-resilient other sectors, the natural protected functions of coastal areas and wetlands must be preserved. This requires the development of institutional capacities at national, regional, and local levels. The government will establish a national coastal area management (NCAM) plan that will be established based on the principles of integrated coastal zone management (ICZM). The NCAM will include the protection and sustainable use of natural coastal areas and wetlands. Governments, through their national and local agencies, will be responsible for the implementation of the NCAM. This will involve the development of a national coastal area management plan that includes the identification of priority areas for conservation and restoration. The government will also develop a national coastal area management plan that includes the identification of priority areas for conservation and restoration.

Following two activities from Activity 1.1 to 3 will be conducted to achieve Result 1:

- **Activity 1.1**: Inventory capacity for risk assessment on present coastal and coral reef conditions
- **Activity 1.2**: Implementation of policy support (ICZM) at the national level
- **Activity 1.3**: Development of a national coastal area management plan

Coastal conservation/protection measures will be implemented at selected areas for shoreline stabilization, including the construction of breakwaters and other structures. These measures will be maintained by established community-based beach maintenance committees. The government will also implement a national coastal protection program.

Following two activities from Activity 2.1 to 3 will be conducted to achieve Result 2:

- **Activity 2.1**: Coastal defense for coastal conservation measures and capacity development of shoreline communities

The system for dissemination of hazards warning/information covering the whole country will be built and capable of disseminating information (hazard maps) for raising the awareness of communities at risk. The government will also establish an early warning system that will be maintained by established community-based beach maintenance committees.

Following two activities from Activity 3.1 to 2 will be conducted to achieve Result 3:

- **Activity 3.1**: Establishment of a national hazard early warning system
- **Activity 3.2**: Development of a national coastal protection plan

### Result 4: Improved dissemination and monitoring of long-term, new, local, coastal, coral reef and other ecosystem services

The government will also develop a national coastal area management plan that includes the identification of priority areas for conservation and restoration. The government will also establish an early warning system that will be maintained by established community-based beach maintenance committees.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL (v2.1) PAGE 34 OF 51

## Overview of Funding Streams and Eligibility Criteria

### Funding Streams

- **Funding Stream A**
  - Description: Provides financial support for projects focusing on renewable energy.
  - Eligibility: Non-profit organizations, government agencies, and private companies.

- **Funding Stream B**
  - Description: Supports initiatives aimed at improving energy efficiency in buildings.
  - Eligibility: Private companies, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), and educational institutions.

- **Funding Stream C**
  - Description: Focuses on funding projects that promote sustainable transportation.
  - Eligibility: Public transport authorities, private transport companies, and research institutions.

### Eligibility Criteria

- **General Requirements**
  - Applicants must have a valid project proposal.
  - Proof of financial stability is required.
  - Projects should align with the green climate funding objectives.

- **Specific Requirements by Stream**

| Funding Stream | Specific Requirements |
| -------------- | --------------------- |
| **A**          | Projects must utilize at least 50% renewable energy sources. |
| **B**          | Buildings must meet LEED certification standards.         |
| **C**          | Projects should reduce carbon emissions by a minimum of 20%. |

### Application Process

1. **Pre-application**
   - Submit a brief project overview.
   - Initial eligibility check.

2. **Full Application**
   - Detailed project proposal.
   - Financial and technical feasibility studies.

3. **Review and Evaluation**
   - Technical review by experts.
   - Financial assessment by financial analysts.

4. **Approval and Funding**
   - Final approval by the funding committee.
   - Disbursement of funds in installments.

### Timeline

- **Pre-application Deadline**: April 15, 20XX
- **Full Application Deadline**: June 30, 20XX
- **Review Period**: July 1 – August 31, 20XX
- **Approval and Funding Disbursement**: September 1 – October 31, 20XX

### Contact Information

For further inquiries, please contact:

- **Email**: info@greenclimatefund.org
- **Phone**: +1 (123) 456-7890
- **Website**: [www.greenclimatefund.org](http://www.greenclimatefund.org)

## B

### 8.1. Projecting future emissions (2020-2050) with representative concentration pathways (RCPs)

The project aims to establish funding for integrated coastal zone management (ICZM). The proposal includes the development of a methodology that integrates the standards while maintaining the quality of data and information. This will be achieved through the use of physical and biological data, measurements, and integrated management approaches appropriate to the needs of the local community.

### Component 1: Establishment of the Integrated Coastal Zone Management (ICZM)

The project development in the island states is the Maldives has been made without sufficient attention to the environmental impact. The project aims to promote sustainable development through the establishment of an integrated coastal zone management system. As of October 2019, the Maldives only has one integrated coastal zone management plan. Money for historical preservation and cultural heritage is not included in this plan.

According to the Climate Change Act, it is recommended to secure at least 30% in their annual budget for climate change adaptation. This act is not clearly defined of structural bodies. It is suggested that house of finance, an existing body within the government, be responsible for managing these funds. The integrated management system will include the development of coastal areas and islands of the Maldives.

The project aims to build on the work done in 2017-2020 of the Maldives where strong positive impacts on environmental protection and disaster risk reduction were observed. These activities are expected to be continued and expanded upon with the support of this proposal.

### Activities

#### Activity 1: Establishment of ICZM along with the development of government policies responsible for the performance of the ICZM

**Activity 1.1:** Development of a national strategy on protected coastal and inland flood reduction measures

- The project will develop a national strategy to reduce flooding in coastal areas.
- This includes the identification of key characteristics, socio-economic conditions, and local environment.
- Several standards are then selected for implementation.

#### Activity 2: Preparation of basic policy of ICZM at the national level

The basic policy for integrated Coastal Zone Management (ICZM) at the national level will be examined. This includes the development of a national strategy on coastal management based on the principles of sustainable development and environmental protection. The national strategy will be developed. Regulation and legal systems necessary for the implementation of ICZM will also be developed. Guidelines (policy issues) will be prepared as the result of this activity.

#### Activity 3: Preparation of concrete ICZM Plan at representative inhabitant island as case study

### Component 2: Implementation of Coastal Conservation/Protection Measures against Coastal Disasters

The objective is to implement and create a coastal conservation/protection measure against coastal disasters in the Maldives. The activities include:

1. **Implementation** of the measures.
2. **Promotion** of the measures.
3. **Monitoring** of the measures.

The measures will be implemented in the 10 selected islands that have been already exposed to coastal disasters. The measures will be promoted through the media and other means. The monitoring will be carried out by the Ministry of Environment and Energy, and the measures will be designed to suit the specific conditions of each island.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## B

### The beach nourishment method is proposed at almost presented three sites as a simple solution.

1. Only the method to solve fundamentally for coastal erosion.
   - There are many hard type measures for coastal erosion, which are hard structure measures such as breakwaters and groynes. These measures are effective in reducing coastal erosion but have negative effects on the environment. Beach nourishment is a soft measure that has less impact on the environment than hard structures. It is also effective in solving coastal erosion problems fundamentally.
   - Multi functions of safety beach
     - Beach nourishment is not only "nourished beach" but also beach use and environment. Restoration of method used and soft is mainly, it is necessary to consider the natural environment and the impact on the local community (1) It is necessary to consider the natural environment and the impact on the local community for the purpose of hard type measures.
     - Natural safety beach has been formed through several thousand years, however, coastal erosion has been accelerated in recent years. Therefore, it is necessary to maintain the natural beach which has been formed over a long period of time.
   - Sustainability (feasibility, flexibility and adaptability) of safety beach
     - It is necessary to consider the sustainability of the beach nourishment method. Continuous supply of sand and natural safety beach are important factors for sustainability. It is also necessary to consider the impact on the local community.

4. Magnitude of beach drift is not cost.
   - Magnitude of beach drift is significantly smaller than that for general coastal erosion measures. Therefore, it is a small scale and it is easy and low cost for hard structure measures such as breakwaters and groynes.

5. Transformation of option development countries for coastal measures
   - Beach nourishment is a method to solve coastal erosion problems fundamentally. It is necessary to consider the transformation from hard measures to soft measures. It is also necessary to consider the impact on the local community.

6. Example of another Projects by JICA.
   - JICA has implemented several projects applying beach nourishment from past cases to be applicable in tropical areas countries with coral coasts. One large scale project is the beach nourishment project in Indonesia. This project was implemented with a sophisticated professional team and international cooperation. As for other projects, technical cooperation by JICA has been implemented in many countries. The sustainability of the method is ensured by strong commitment of Maldivian Governments and communities after the implementation.

# Introduction of Small-Scale Projects and Relation to This Project

## Overview

Green Climate Fund (GCF) project is a small-scale beach nourishment project that has maintained about 20 years of sufficient coastal and environmental management. It is one of the smallest projects in the GCF portfolio.

### 1. Outline

#### 1.1. Project Implementation

A commitment (cash grant) was undertaken to recover the cost of sandy beach against coastal erosion. Further, production of small-scale GCF was carried out by the government. The quality of sand for beach nourishment is about 300,000 m³ at Nusa Dua and about 150,000 m³ at Sanur.

#### 1.2. The Blockade

The blockade has been in place since initial beach nourishment at Sanur and Nusa Dua, and 14 years ago at Kuta Beach. The blockade was also used to maintain the effectiveness of beach nourishment (including less than 20 years of expected planning and design).

### 2. Initial Condition after the Project

#### 2.1. Project Implementation

The project was implemented for about 3 years at Sanur and Nusa Dua without sufficient management (physical, sand replenishment). The impact is proven that nourished sand could not be maintained.

On the other hand, underdeveloped beach management has been recently observed, which is a problem of the lack of maintenance and the technical team.

## B

www.csi.nyc.gov. New York Beach Conservation Project has been ongoing since the year 2018 and establishment of the project is to maintain the natural habitat of the beach.

### Similarly to this Project, Relevant Outside Outcome

The similarity between BCFP and the proposed project for the Maldives in the funding proposal is as follows:

- BCFP was the first experience to employ beach nourishment method to widen public coastline as a means to combat coastal erosion.
- The project has been successful in achieving its goals of widening the beach while maintaining the natural habitat.
- There has been a lot of discussion on the topic of "beach nourishment" and "artificial beach" and whether it is environmentally friendly or not. However, the project has been completed with minimal environmental impact and with the community's overall approval such as public hearings and local government support.
- The Maldives project also employs beach nourishment to combat coastal erosion and has already made significant progress in the project. BCFP was already able to teach and this method is also recommended in the proposed project for the Maldives.

All beach nourishment is a cheaper and safer way to maintain the original natural beaches. It is not available to all countries, but it is a viable option if the country has the resources and expertise to do so. If used to maintain a protected area every year, it can be a cost-effective solution for maintaining the coastline. The Maldives project is an example of this. Some category may benefit from this project as well.

The New York Beach Conservation Project was completed with the help of local experts in beach nourishment and has been successful in achieving its goals. The project has also been completed by the government and has been supported by the community.

- Coastal erosion is a major threat to coastal communities.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## PAGE 4 OF 8

In other words, "soft measures" is to protect coasts by not only hard structures but also by beach nourishment and sand replenishment. Structures such as groynes, sand dunes, and sand fences are used to trap sand and maintain the natural sediment budget of a coastal system. Sand is often mined from offshore sand deposits or dredged from shipping channels. These measures are often combined with other measures such as beach nourishment, sand replenishment, and sand fencing.

### Introduction

In many cases, when soft measures are used, there are many benefits, which are "physical" like beach and "ecological" like beach. The former is just in terms of sand on beaches such as my garden. The latter is the ecological value of the beach to wildlife. This includes the following:

- Sand dunes
- Beach grass
- Sand fences

These measures can be used to protect the beach from erosion and maintain the natural sediment budget of a coastal system. They are often combined with other measures such as sand replenishment, sand fencing, and sand dune restoration.

### Table 1: Hard (Structural) Measures vs Soft Measures

| Hard (Structural) Measures | Soft Measures |
|---------------------------|---------------|
| Groynes                  | Beach nourishment |
| Breakwaters              | Sand replenishment |
| Sea walls                 | Sand fencing    |
| Moles or groins           | Sand dune restoration |
| Rock armours              | Sand dune planting |
| (Artificial reef)         | By concrete structures |
| Riprap                   | Natural sand dune management |
| Sand bags                 | Beach grass planting |
| Sand fences               | Sand dune restoration |
| Sand dunes                | Sand dune transplantation |
| Marram grass planting     | Sand dune stabilization |
| Sand dune restoration     | Sand dune stabilization system, ENS, etc. |

Source: IUCN

SMC mentioned that though the initial cost of beach nourishment is cheaper than other hard structure measures, the maintenance cost of beach nourishment is assured 20 years in significant height and width. The cost of sand replenishment can be maintained in every 5 years and particular annual replenishment with same quality of total beach width can be maintained for 10 years. Therefore, the cost of soft measures is cheaper than hard structure measures under the regular sand discharge and required frequency and quantity of sand for maintenance will also maintain.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL

## B

Beach (Blue/Green beach nourishment) at Insulgoot beach and the Maldives has been implemented by Japan International Cooperation Agency. If proper method of beach nourishment is employed while maintaining environmental standards, it can be a cost-effective solution for coastal protection.

1. Beach nourishment can reduce human activities and risk of erosion.
2. Beach nourishment can improve the quality of life in coastal communities.
3. Beach nourishment can be used as an effective tool for understanding of coastal processes, beach dynamics, and coastal hazards.

### Differences of Beach Nourishment between by Government and by Private Sectors

- **Purpose of beach nourishment is different** between the government and private sectors. Government projects are generally funded from public funds and aim to protect public beaches, which benefit the local community. Private sector projects are often funded through commercial activities such as tourism, real estate development, or other business interests. The purpose of these projects may include:
  - **Protection of public assets**: Government-funded beach nourishment is often used to protect public infrastructure, such as roads and buildings.
  - **Economic benefits**: Private sector projects may be funded by commercial property owners who want to maintain the value of their investments.

- **Funding sources are different** between government and private sectors. Government projects are typically funded through public funds, while private sector projects are funded through commercial activities.

### Applicability of Beach Nourishment in the Maldives

1. **Promote beach nourishment**: The Maldives can promote beach nourishment to enhance the quality of beaches and improve tourism.
2. **Environmental impact assessment**: Conduct an environmental impact assessment to ensure that beach nourishment does not harm marine life or coral reefs.
3. **Community involvement**: Engage local communities in the planning and implementation process to ensure that beach nourishment is sustainable and benefits all stakeholders.

### Activity 2.3: Implementation of beach maintenance, establishment of structure and capacity development of stakeholders

The overall environmental management is essential with staff changes to local entities as essential in order to sustainably maintain the green beaches after the beach nourishment carried out in Activity 2.2.

1. **Environmental Management:** The local government will be responsible for the environmental management of the beaches in accordance with the change of beaches, which will ensure active and healthy beaches.
2. **Staff Training:** JICA will maintain the good condition of the beaches by training local staff on environmental protection and conservation. This includes training on beach maintenance and conservation practices in cooperation with NGOs to enhance the sustainable management of the beaches.
3. **Capacity Development:** JICA will support the capacity development of local government officials and communities for beach maintenance by conducting the actual activities after the completion of the project.

An integrated system, which includes monitoring, evaluation, feedback, plan and action, is required to achieve management for nourished beaches as shown in Figure 47. JICA will contribute to the establishment of an integrated system that includes monitoring, evaluation, feedback, planning and action. This will include public education, government policy, private sector involvement for sustainable beach maintenance.

### Component 3: Development of Disaster Warning and Information Dissemination

Considering the remaining risk of extreme disasters that cannot be fully mitigated by structural measures, it is necessary to develop a system for disseminating disaster warning and information. In order for the general public to take preventive actions in advance, it is essential to establish an effective early warning system.

## Component B: Development of Bio Data Collection and Sharing System Related to Climate Change

### Introduction
In a timely manner, this component, co-financed by GCA, will lead a system for the collection of bio-data from the target communities. This system will be designed to ensure that the data collected is appropriate to operate the system, easy to collect, reliable, and appropriate for decision-making purposes.

### Early Warning Broadcast System (EWBS)
Early Warning Broadcast System (EWBS) will be set up on the basis of integrated sensors (mobile broadcasting). Networking systems will be installed in each village. This new technology enables people to receive early warnings about climate change and other disasters through mobile phones. The system will be designed to provide information to the community, especially women and children.

### Component 1: Development of Bio Data Collection System
This component is also expected to lead a system with SDBP project under existing "Green Rainwater Management" in the Maldives. Establishments have neither chance of understanding each greenhouse gas based on factual data, nor the means to understand the impact of climate change and the data of external factors or weather and sea levels, which are the most relevant cause of the increase of coastal disaster risk due to climate change. The establishment has no means to understand the impact of climate change based on quantitative information and continuous study of the countermeasures against climate change.

This component, funded by JICA, assists Goldi with a system for obtaining and sharing-based data related to climate change. The system is designed to be used by local governments and communities through technical assistance from the government. The government agrees to sufficiently and constantly share the necessary information and data with the community. The government will implement the following two activities (see Figure 49).

A long-term system and a real-time monitoring system will be developed and necessary technical transfer can be provided in order to obtain the long-term academic framework related to climate change. Three regional units will be established to support the implementation of the system and achieve low-level objectives.

This component is divided into two types of monitoring systems: long-term monitoring for coastal areas, coral reefs, and sea level monitoring systems. The monitoring system applies satellite images and GIS systems that will be developed in order to monitor changes in coastal areas, coral reefs, and sea levels. The data obtained from the monitoring system can be applied to the planning process of changes of beach profile and local impact at specific areas such as coastal erosion.

### Methods of Compliance

#### Overview

The project aims to reduce carbon emissions by implementing renewable energy sources and improving energy efficiency.

#### Project Components

- **Component 1: Renewable Energy Implementation**
  - **Objective**: To increase the share of renewable energy in the national grid.
  - **Activities**:
    - Installation of solar panels
    - Construction of wind farms
    - Development of hydroelectric power plants

- **Component 2: Energy Efficiency Enhancement**
  - **Objective**: To reduce energy consumption by improving efficiency.
  - **Activities**:
    - Implementation of LED lighting systems
    - Upgrading to energy-efficient appliances
    - Building insulation and weatherization programs

#### Risk Management Strategy

- **Risk Identification and Analysis**
  - Identify potential risks using a risk matrix.
  - Analyze the impact and likelihood of each risk.

- **Risk Mitigation Measures**
  - Develop contingency plans for high-impact risks.
  - Implement regular monitoring to detect early signs of risk.

#### Monitoring and Evaluation

- **Baseline Data Collection**
  - Collect baseline data on current, used, and unused energy sources.
  - Establish a database for ongoing monitoring.

- **Monitoring Framework**
  - Set up a system for continuous monitoring.
  - Use remote sensing technology to track progress.

- **Reporting Mechanism**
  - Regularly update stakeholders on project status.
  - Publish annual reports detailing achievements and challenges.

#### Stakeholder Engagement

- **Community Involvement**
  - Engage local communities in planning and implementation.
  - Provide training and capacity building for community members.

- **Private Sector Participation**
  - Encourage private sector investment in renewable energy projects.
  - Facilitate public-private partnerships to enhance project outcomes.

#### Financial Management

- **Budget Allocation**
  - Allocate funds based on project needs and priorities.
  - Ensure transparency and accountability in financial management.

- **Funding Sources**
  - Secure funding from international donors and development agencies.
  - Explore innovative financing mechanisms such as green bonds.

## Implementation Arrangements (Page 8 of 10)

### Implementation Arrangement

Component 1: The project will be implemented through Component 1 as a non-physical measure and Component 2 as a physical measure. Component 1 will be implemented by the Environmental Department of ME in collaboration with the National Designation Authority (NDA) and the Ministry of Environment (ME). Component 2 will be implemented by the Project Management Unit (PMU) on behalf of ME, with a start-up Activity 2.1 (Including baseline survey, design, procurement, and supervision). The PMU will be responsible for the overall implementation of the project. The activities under Activity 2.1 will be carried out in close coordination with the NDA and Component 1, and a team under the JCAE funding will be formed to support the implementation of Component 2. The activities under Activity 2.1 and Activity 2.3, and Component 3, will be executed by the JCAE funding unit.

Component 2: The project will be implemented through Component 2 as a physical measure. Component 2 will be implemented by the Project Management Unit (PMU) on behalf of ME, with a start-up Activity 2.1 (Including baseline survey, design, procurement, and supervision). The PMU will be responsible for the overall implementation of the project. The activities under Activity 2.1 and Activity 2.3, and Component 3, will be executed by the JCAE funding unit.

### Project Steering Committee (PSC)

The PSC will oversee the planning, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of the project. It will consist of representatives from relevant ministries, agencies, and other stakeholders. The PSC will meet at least once a year to review the progress of the project and provide guidance on its implementation. The PSC will be chaired by the Minister of Environment (ME) or his/her representative.

### Executing Entity (EE)

The EE for Component 2 will be implemented by the Environmental Department of ME in collaboration with the NDA and the Ministry of Environment (ME). The EE will be responsible for the overall implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of activities. The EE will be supported by the PMU, which will be responsible for the day-to-day management of the project. The EE will implement the activities under Component 2 in close coordination with the NDA and Component 1.

### JCAE and Component 3

JCAE and Component 3 will be implemented by JCAE's Project Team that will be formed for this purpose. The team will be responsible for the overall implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of activities under Component 3. The team will report to the EE and provide advice and assistance to the PSC in the consideration of the project.

### Project Management Unit (PMU)

The PMU, assigned on behalf of ME for Component 2, is responsible for the day-to-day activity implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of the project. The PMU will be headed by a Project Manager appointed by ME. The PMU will be responsible for the overall management of the project and will report to the EE.

Gold has spent USD 30 million of government budget and USD 5 million from international aid agencies funding climate change mitigation projects in the Maldives. These projects are in an advanced stage, although GCF has disbursed a budget of USD 4.83 million to UNDP for climate change adaptation projects in the Maldives.

Gold is committed to working with the government and other stakeholders to ensure that the funds are used effectively and transparently. The government has been supportive of Gold's efforts, and the two parties have agreed to collaborate on future projects. Gold will continue to work closely with the government and other stakeholders to ensure that the funds are used effectively and transparently.

To implement comprehensive climate change mitigation measures and to the results to these appropriate measures in the event disaster strikes an infrastructure, the allocation of conventional resources is necessary. The Maldives has a unique geographical structure which makes it vulnerable to climate change impacts. It is necessary to plan properly for the implementation of coastal adaptation/mitigation measures in the Maldives. This includes the development of a comprehensive coastal management plan. At the same time, it is also important to develop a comprehensive disaster risk management plan that will help to reduce the impact of disasters on the country's infrastructure and economy.

The Government of Maldives is preparing the County Assistance Program for the Maldives, in which the Government of Japan is participating as a major donor. The Government of Maldives has also developed a comprehensive climate change adaptation strategy with the support of UNDP.

Based on the above decision of the Government of Maldives and GCA, the role of JICA assistance in the project would be as the coordinator that will consider the integrated implementation of development and climate change mitigation measures. The Government of Japan has been a long-term partner of the Maldives in disaster risk reduction (Disaster Risk Reduction Component 3), and the component of cooperation between the two countries is an important element for the success of this project. The Government of Japan will also provide technical assistance to the Maldives in the field of climate change adaptation.

In the Maldives, the experience of JICA was pointed out by the last through studies and pilot projects conducted on climate change mitigation measures. ## B

Amount of the international self-agreements as of September 2018 is the Coastal Protection Program at the North Sea (Küstenschutzprogramm) and the National Adaptation Strategy (Nationale Anpassungsstrategie). The aim of both programs is to adapt to climate change. The following section explains in detail measures from our institution as well as agreements with other institutions.

Because of climate change impact, coastal areas in the Netherlands are exposed to increasing risks. These risks have been identified and assessed by local authorities. Under these circumstances, the GCF funds in this project will be used for the implementation of a coastal protection program. This program includes the construction of dykes, dams or appropriate land and water management plans, which reduce damage from flooding. The measures are designed to protect the environment and contribute to flood risk reduction. In addition, the measures will include the construction of sand dunes, the removal of sand from the sea bed of the North Sea, the improvement of coastal protection and the construction of sand dunes.

### B.1. Key staging and sustainability issues (SMS) workstream responsibility

The activities in the project are designed to have studies initiated by planning, designing, implementing and monitoring. The key staging and sustainability issues are reported for ensuring the sustainability of the project.

1. **For implementation of the ICZM protocol**
   - The draft protocols developed for the large islands of the project, identified as necessary for practicing integrated coastal zone management (ICZM). The protocol will be implemented in a two-year period. To start the formalized process, the ICZM guidelines at the national level are a guide.
   - The implementation of the protocol will include the development of the national protocols and the development of the local protocols. The local protocols will be developed by the local authorities involved in the project. The local authorities will be responsible for the implementation of the protocol during the implementation of the case studies in the common areas.

2. **For continued maintenance of nourished beaches**
   - The continued maintenance of the nourished beaches is necessary to ensure the sustainability of the nourished beaches. Maintenance manuals will be prepared, which include guidelines for sand collection and its application. The basis of the manual will be the results of the monitoring activities that are carried out during the project.
   - Domestic authorities responsible for the nourishment will be identified and the agreed maintenance plan will be developed. The lessons learned from the pilot projects will be used to develop a maintenance plan for the nourished beaches. Capacity building and technical activities are planned in Activity 2.1 and 2.2.

### Activity 2.1: Implementation of coastal management measures and capacity development for the establishment of a system of integrated coastal zone management (ICZM) by PMA.
The same consultant will prepare the plans for the activity management systems and community-based monitoring, and will also provide training on the use of these plans. The consultant will be responsible for the design of the beach nourishment and its implementation plan after the consultation in Activity 2.2. The output based of this activity is the establishment of a system of integrated coastal zone management (ICZM) by PMA. This activity will be implemented by the ME, MPA, local government, and community for them to disseminate knowledge and we will also provide training on how to implement these activities.

### Activity 2.2: Implementation of beach nourishment; establishment of structure and capacity development of integrated coastal zone management (ICZM) at the national level.
The same consultants above will prepare the plans for the activity management systems and community-based monitoring, and will also provide training on the use of these plans. The consultant will be responsible for the design of the beach nourishment and its implementation plan after the consultation in Activity 2.1. The output based of this activity is the establishment of a system of integrated coastal zone management (ICZM) at the national level. The local consultants hired as sub-engineers of the consultant who will participate in the training, will be responsible for the design and construction supervision. This will help to build capacity and experience and to support the field consultancy team.

The GCF financing fund to be used for these activities is the expenses for the consultant for detailed design and construction supervision, which is approximately USD 3.4 million.

# EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

## D. Expected performance (500 words maximum)

This proposal outlines how the project will perform against the investment criteria set out in the [Green Climate Fund Investment Criteria](https://www.gcfund.org/investment-criteria).

### 3.1 Impact

The project aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by at least 20% compared to business-as-usual scenarios, and at enhanced levels against climate change, offering the native forest benefits of carbon and water while enhancing biodiversity. The project will also contribute to reducing poverty through sustainable management systems and up-scaling the project. In the context of ICZM that aims to enhance the resilience of coastal communities, the project is expected to bring significant improvements in the environment, effective and sustainable land and coastal management.

The same benefits at the local level are the best means of the component of the ICZM capacity to be implemented by the government. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs). The project will also contribute to the implementation of the Paris Agreement.

Pollution measures will be implemented, the coasts facing multiple coastal erosion risks are high-risk areas that need to be protected. The area will be integrated into the national system for monitoring and reporting on environmental performance or greenhouse gas emissions. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs).

Implementation of these components that include relevant afforestation, public, civil, and private stakeholders, and the government will be integrated into the national system for monitoring and reporting on environmental performance or greenhouse gas emissions. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs). The project will also contribute to the implementation of the Paris Agreement.

### 3.2 Financial sustainability

The project is expected to be financially sustainable, with a payback period of less than five years. This is based on the assumption that the project will generate sufficient revenue to cover its operating costs and provide a return on investment for the investors. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs).

### 3.3 Additionality

According to Second National Communications to the Maldives in 2016, due to the negative experience of the first phase of the project, the government has not been able to implement the project at the current stage. Uncertainty and lack of measurement mainly relate to difficulties in implementing the project. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs).

This project interventions aim to shift the paradigm away from current common understanding and practice towards a more integrated approach that combines both natural and social sciences. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs). The project will also contribute to the implementation of the Paris Agreement.

## Context for coastal zone protection and long-term sustainable management

To alleviate the loss of coastal wetlands and mangroves, and to protect the livelihoods of people in these areas, it is recommended that the maximum mitigation of coastal erosion cases experienced by the county should be achieved.

### Extent for activities use and adaptation

The proposal will include a comprehensive CZM planning and design for building natural infrastructure based on the plan and strengthening of the existing waterfront development. Additionally, the proposal will be applied to other sites including the restoration of degraded wetlands and mangroves. Specifically, the proposal will focus on restoring coastal wetlands and degraded mangrove forests through the use of nature-based solutions. The activities will include the planting of native species in degraded areas, the establishment of natural infrastructure such as spawning grounds for fish, and the promotion of sustainable practices by local communities.

The proposal will also include the development of a comprehensive monitoring system to track the progress of the project. This will involve the use of remote sensing technologies and other methods to monitor the health of the wetlands and mangroves over time. The proposal will also include the establishment of a community-based monitoring system, which will allow local communities to participate in the monitoring process.

### Principles for sustainable funding and finance

One of the causes of coastal erosion that have occurred in the Maldives is the difference in basic perceptions and lack of information among the concerned organizations involved in coastal resource management. Therefore, it is important to develop a comprehensive approach to coastal resource management that includes the involvement of local communities and other stakeholders.

The proposal will also include the development of a comprehensive monitoring system to track the progress of the project. This will involve the use of remote sensing technologies and other methods to monitor the health of the wetlands and mangroves over time. The proposal will also include the establishment of a community-based monitoring system, which will allow local communities to participate in the monitoring process.

### Contribution to the resilience framework and policies

The proposal will contribute to the development of a comprehensive approach to coastal resource management that includes the involvement of local communities and other stakeholders.

The ICZM plan will be formulated as a part of the comprehensive study of the larger island and its interconnections with the mainland. It is expected that the plan will develop necessary laws and regulations to improve environmental and social conditions on the island. The plan will also provide guidelines for the implementation of the ICZM plan.

The establishment of an integrated coastal management system is expected to contribute to the plan and shape methods for coastal structure measures to be implemented in a sustainable manner. This will help to ensure that the needs of all stakeholders are met, including local communities, businesses, and government agencies.

Overall, environmental issues include natural resources, disaster management, climate change adaptation strategies, and other related issues.

The establishment of the project on the island document for a national ICZM will show the direction for the implementation of the above activities to adapt to climate change. The plan will also provide guidelines for the development of coastal structures that are resilient to climate change. The plan will also include measures to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and develop a climate resilient economy.

#### 11.c. Support local developed countries, including through financial and technical assistance, in building their capacity to implement this target.

### 12. By 2030, enhance the sustainability management and efficient use of natural resources, including through the development of sustainable infrastructure and providing access to equal opportunities for women and girls.

#### 12.c. Enhance support for developing countries on improving their scientific and technological capacity to move towards more sustainable patterns of consumption and production.

### 13. By 2030, double the amount of natural areas covered by protected areas compared to that in 2010, including through a significant increase in the area-covered by other effective area-based conservation measures.

#### 13.a. Increase the area-covered by protected areas and other effective area-based conservation measures to at least 17% of terrestrial and inland water areas and 10% of coastal and marine areas.

### d. Needs for recurrent costs, 800 credits, approximately 1 year

The baseline is a broad funding development goal (BDG) highly vulnerable to climate change and conflict. The commercial coastal protection measures by applying soft structures have been common in the Netherlands for decades. The Dutch Delta Commission has recommended that the government should consider the demands for development. GEF plans to support the right of land communities to protect their natural environment, including the ecosystem services provided by the important and biodiversity areas as well as to promote the sustainable use of natural resources.

In each year, GEF allocates approximately USD 5 million to USD 11 million for coastal protection with a special focus on developing countries. The GEF has supported the development of nature-based coastal adaptation measures, including beach nourishment and sand dune restoration. Examples of nature-based solutions include mangrove restoration in Indonesia, coral reef restoration in the Maldives, and beach nourishment in the Philippines.

The GEF is also working to promote the use of integrated approaches to address challenges considering the sustainability of the project to expand to other vulnerable areas.

Many countries to develop GEF funding a national level and country plans for green and coastal natural protection. The GEF has supported the development of national beach nourishment and coral reef restoration projects in many developing countries, including the Philippines, Indonesia, and the Maldives. The GEF is also working to promote the use of integrated approaches to address challenges considering the sustainability of the project to expand to other vulnerable areas.

Sharing best practices with private sector partners can help to promote sustainable coastal protection measures. GEF and partners can provide support for the private sector of special needs equipment such as dredging, working vessels, etc., and management which are required to physical.

### Integrated National Environmental Compliance (INECOMP)

INECOMP is a legal policy document for climate change mitigation and adaptation in the Republic of Kenya. It takes into account the resilience of infrastructure, climate innovation, and climate change adaptation measures.
