# National Adaptation Program of Action (NAPA) (2013)

This program shares a framework to climate change adaptation and disaster risk management in the Maldives. It includes the implementation of land use planning policies, protection of natural resources, prevention of coastal erosion, with both soft and hard measures; prevention of critical infrastructure, and the preservation of sea level rise.

# Green Climate Change Policy Framework (2016)

This framework aims to promote activities in NAPA to adapt to climate change. It also includes the implementation of the Maldives National Adaptation Program of Action (NAPA).

# Japan-Caribbean Climate Change Partnership (JCCCP) 2017

This is a revised version of the current administration starting from November 2016, placing high emphasis on preserving the island's Maldivian island and atoll environment.

The Maldives also intends to establish closer quality control measures and monitoring systems for the project. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL (V.2.1) PAGE 8 OF 9

## E: Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (568 words, 1 page)

### Objective

Through technological advances regarding data management, monitoring, data is increasingly automated.

### Evaluation criteria

- **4.2.1 Development of sale-use monitoring systems** using satellite imagery and GIS technology.
- **4.2.2 Development of monitoring system** including remote sensing and field measurements.

### Implementation

The project will be monitored and evaluated based on the key indicators described from E.4 to E.6. The evaluation will be conducted by an independent third party, as per the proposal. The results will be reflected by reviewing the detailed design over the first two years of implementation. Results indicators will be:

- **4.2.1 Development of sale-use monitoring systems** using satellite imagery and GIS technology.
- **4.2.2 Development of monitoring system** including remote sensing and field measurements.

The components of the proposal are monitored and evaluated by E.6 of each component. The component to be implemented by GOF fund will be monitored and evaluated on a regular basis.

System evaluation will be implemented with three years and five months from the commencement of implementation. The implementation of the project will be reviewed in accordance with the following criteria:

- **4.2.1 Development of sale-use monitoring systems** using satellite imagery and GIS technology.
- **4.2.2 Development of monitoring system** including remote sensing and field measurements.

Evaluation results will include necessary conditions (pre for initial evaluation, assessment of implementation progress, and impact on the environment). The evaluation results will be used to improve the project's effectiveness and efficiency, and impacts and lessons learned (only for final evaluation).

## 3. GOVERNANCE POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### 3.1 Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF)

The ESMF is a comprehensive set of policies, procedures, guidelines, and standards that will be used to ensure compliance with the Green Climate Fund's environmental and social safeguards. The ESMF will be developed in consultation with relevant stakeholders and will include:

- A risk management framework
- Environmental and social impact assessments
- Grievance mechanisms

### 3.2 Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP)

The ESMP is a detailed plan that outlines the specific measures to be taken to avoid or minimize adverse environmental and social impacts of the project. The ESMP will include:

- An environmental management plan
- A social management plan
- A gender equality action plan

### 3.3 Natural Environmental Considerations

Natural environmental considerations are essential to ensure that the project does not have a negative impact on the environment. This includes:

- Conducting an environmental impact assessment (EIA)
- Implementing mitigation measures
- Monitoring and evaluating the environmental impacts of the project

### 3.4 Cultural Heritage Management

Cultural heritage management is important to protect cultural sites and artifacts that may be affected by the project. This includes:

- Conducting a cultural heritage impact assessment (CHIA)
- Implementing mitigation measures
- Monitoring and evaluating the cultural heritage impacts of the project

### 3.5 Biodiversity Management

Biodiversity management is essential to ensure that the project does not have a negative impact on biodiversity. This includes:

- Conducting a biodiversity impact assessment (BIA)
- Implementing mitigation measures
- Monitoring and evaluating the biodiversity impacts of the project

### 3.6 Indigenous Peoples Considerations

Indigenous peoples considerations are important to ensure that the rights and interests of indigenous peoples are protected. This includes:

- Conducting free, prior, and informed consent (FPIC) processes
- Implementing grievance mechanisms
- Monitoring and evaluating the impacts on indigenous peoples

### 3.7 Gender Equality and Social Inclusion

Gender equality and social inclusion are essential to ensure that the project benefits all members of society. This includes:

- Conducting gender and social impact assessments (GSIAs)
- Implementing mitigation measures
- Monitoring and evaluating the gender and social impacts of the project

### 3.8 Stakeholder Engagement

Stakeholder engagement is important to ensure that the project has the support of all relevant stakeholders. This includes:

- Conducting stakeholder consultations
- Implementing grievance mechanisms
- Monitoring and evaluating stakeholder engagement processes

### 3.9 Grievance Mechanisms

Grievance mechanisms are essential to provide a means for affected persons to raise concerns and seek redress. This includes:

- Establishing a grievance mechanism
- Ensuring that the grievance mechanism is accessible and effective
- Monitoring and evaluating the grievance mechanism

### 3.10 Monitoring and Evaluation

Monitoring and evaluation are essential to ensure that the project is implemented effectively and achieves its objectives. This includes:

- Developing a monitoring and evaluation plan
- Implementing monitoring and evaluation activities
- Reporting on progress and results


---
**Page 90**
---

# Social Considerations

The proposed components seek to reducing vulnerability of the communities against natural disaster and climate change, hence long term positive social impacts expected. Adverse social impact included from the project implementation will be minimized through proper planning and management.

The proposed components do not require mandatory resettlement or acquisition of private lands though some displacement may occur due to infrastructure development. The affected families will be properly compensated according to the government policy. The project will also ensure that the local communities are involved in the project implementation including the project sites along the coasts as volunteer labor.

It is obvious that there is limited impact on communities, consultations will be undertaken to engage local communities and stakeholders in the planning process. The participation of people, regardless of gender, will be encouraged as actual actors for construction works to improve their livelihoods. The project will also ensure that the local communities are involved in the monitoring and evaluation of the project.

## a) Gender Considerations

Gender considerations need to be integrated into all stages of the project cycle from planning through implementation to delivery and impact assessment. This is important to ensure gender equality and to avoid negative impacts on women and girls. The project will also promote equal opportunities for men and women in employment, training and capacity building.

## b) Community Participation

Community participation is an integral part of the project implementation. It is essential that local communities are involved in all stages of planning, design, implementation, operation and maintenance of the project. This will ensure that the project meets the needs of the community and is sustainable over time.

## c) Gender Mainstreaming

Gender mainstreaming is a process of assessing the implications for women and men of any planned action including legislation, policies or programs in all sectors and at all levels. It is a strategy for making the concerns and experiences of women as well as men an integral dimension of the design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of policies and programs so that both women and men benefit equally.

## d) Gender Equality

Gender equality will be promoted through the project by ensuring equal access to resources and opportunities for both women and men. This includes providing training, promoting on-farm diversification and sustainable land management practices.

## e) Gender Analysis

A gender analysis of the project will be conducted to identify any potential gender-related impacts of the project. This will include an assessment of the needs and priorities of different groups within the community.

## f) Gender Training

Training will be provided to both men and women on gender equality and the importance of involving women in decision-making processes. This will help to ensure that the project is implemented in a way that is sensitive to the needs of all members of the community.

## Procurement Guidelines

### Terms, Transparency, and Accountability
All the procedures for all procurement shall use the GCP funding guidelines. All procurement must follow the procurement policies of JICA as well, and the policy will be applied to the procurement by the PMU. In case of any conflict between the procurement policies of GCF and JICA, the procurement policies of JICA will prevail. The senior procurement adviser at the PMU will confirm that the procurement is in line with the procurement policies of both GCF and JICA.

### G. Disclosure of Funding Proposal
The funding proposal shall be disclosed to the public through the following channels:

- **JICA website:** The funding proposal shall be posted on the JICA website.
- **GCF website:** The funding proposal shall be posted on the GCF website.
- **Public hearing:** A public hearing shall be held for the disclosure of the funding proposal.

29 November 2020

Mr. Yasmick Glomance
Executive Director

77A, Avenue International Business District

Abidjan, Côte d'Ivoire

Re: Funding proposal for the "GCF in Japan International Cooperation Agency (JICA) Building Cleaner Resilience Safeshelters in Madagascar"

Dear Mr. Glomance,

We refer to the project Building Cleaner Resilience Safeshelters in Madagascar as included in the funding proposal submitted by Japan International Cooperation Agency to us on 24 November 2020.

The Government of the Republic of Madagascar, through the Ministry of Environment, Ecology and Sustainable Development, hereby communicates its no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal.

To communicate our no objection, it is requested that:

- The Government of the Republic of Madagascar, through the Ministry of Environment, Ecology and Sustainable Development, hereby communicates its no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP165

**Proposal name:** Building Climate Resilient Infrastructure in the Maldives

**Accredited entity:** Japan International Cooperation Agency (JICA)

**Country(ies):** Republic of Maldives

**Proposed/preparer name:** Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### 1. Strengths
The proposal is the GF country Environmental and Social Safeguards Management System (ESSMS) for the Ministry of Environment and Energy. The project aims to enhance capacity for the implementation of environmental and social safeguard policies, promote community ownership and in alignment with the country’s development priorities.

The proposed action is to address the barriers to the creation of a robust ESSMS through the establishment of an institutional framework and guidelines during the initial stages of developing the system. The project will also support the integration of environmental and social safeguards into the planning, design, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation processes for climate resilient infrastructure projects.

The Board was provided with additional information on the proposed action through a presentation by the preparer. The Board’s discussion focused on the importance of integrating environmental and social safeguards into the project cycle to ensure that the project is implemented in an environmentally and socially sustainable manner.

### 2. Points of clarity
The proposed estimate is highly dependent on active operations and administrative activities during the implementation period. The proposed action aims to enhance the capacity of the Ministry of Environment and Energy to implement environmental and social safeguards for climate resilient infrastructure projects. The proposed action will involve the development of an institutional framework, guidelines, and tools to support the integration of environmental and social safeguards into the project cycle.

The proposed action is to address the barriers to the creation of a robust ESSMS through the establishment of an institutional framework and guidelines during the initial stages of developing the system. The project will also support the integration of environmental and social safeguards into the planning, design, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation processes for climate resilient infrastructure projects.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background
The Republic of Maldives consists of 26 atolls and 1,192 islands within the range of equatorial waters in the Indian Ocean. The islands of the Maldives are divided into two main groups: the northern chain and the southern chain. The total land area is approximately 300 square kilometers, with a population density of about 1,400 people per square kilometer.

The Maldives is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as sea-level rise, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, and changes in precipitation patterns. The country’s low elevation and limited land area make it particularly susceptible to the effects of climate change.

The Maldives are particularly vulnerable to climate change because they are so close to sea level. The Maldives have been identified as one of the countries most at risk from the impacts of global warming and rising sea levels.

# Implementation of each maintenance, establishment of structure and capacity development of establishments

## General description

### Project title: GEF Small Grant Programme (SGP) (2019-2023)

### Project ID: SGP-GEF-048

The system for dissemination of cleaner writing/printing information should be both effective and efficient. The system will be implemented through the following activities:

i. Identify the components, the following two activities will be conducted:
   - Introduction of environmental digital management systems, and
   - Development of a training manual on environmental management.

ii. This component is funded by the AF. There is no GEF funding requirement for this component.

### Project description

#### Component A: Dissemination of Cleaner Writing/Printing Collection and Awareness raising related to climate change (SGP-GEF-048, SGP-GEF-049)

This component will include the following activities:
   - Development of a system for dissemination of cleaner writing/printing information.
   - Development of a training manual on environmental management systems.

#### Component B: Establishment of waste and waste water treatment systems (SGP-GEF-050, SGP-GEF-051)

This component will include the following activities:
   - Development of waste and waste water treatment systems.
   - Development of a monitoring system for waste and waste water treatment systems.

### Project objectives

#### Component A: Dissemination of Cleaner Writing/Printing Collection and Awareness raising related to climate change (SGP-GEF-048, SGP-GEF-049)

The GEF grant financing represents 1.4 per cent of the total project investment costs. The GEF grant investment amounts to 4.7 per cent of the total grant amount requested from the AF.

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential

Scale: Medium to high

i. Mathew's expanded significant cluster works of which three units and will cover an area of 20 hectares, and will create jobs for 50 people in the project site.
   - The major source of different cost items is from the local government, and from 60 per cent to 100 per cent in the project site.
   - Other activities include the integration of the core environmental and social safeguards activities. As a result, climate change mitigation measures will be implemented through the following activities: waste management, water treatment, and soil conservation.

ii. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the following GEF strategic objectives:
   - Reduction in greenhouse gas emissions.
   - Improvement in livelihoods.
   - Enhancement of ecosystem services.

# The proposal seeks to mitigate the impacts of climate risks using cost-effective and sustainable climate-adaptive protective measures.

The selected interventions are scheduled for implementation in two phases over a period of five years. Phase I will be financed with GCF funds. The islands of Abai Anga and Ada Afoah, located in the Maldives, will be protected from coastal erosion through the construction of breakwaters and revetments.

## 3.1

## GF X.Y.Z

### a) Project title

**Title:** Enhancing coastal resilience through integrated ecosystem-based approaches in Madagascar (L3 medium)

### b) Project description

Madagascar is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts, particularly sea-level rise and increased frequency of extreme weather events. The project will support the Government of Madagascar to enhance the resilience of coastal ecosystems and communities to climate change by promoting integrated ecosystem-based approaches for coastal protection and adaptation. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the country's Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) targets.

### c) Project objectives

The project aims to:

- Enhance the resilience of coastal ecosystems and communities to climate change impacts.
- Promote integrated ecosystem-based approaches for coastal protection and adaptation.
- Support the Government of Madagascar in achieving its NDCs targets.

### d) Project components

The project will be implemented through three main components:

1. **Component 1:** Integrated coastal management planning
2. **Component 2:** Ecosystem restoration and enhancement
3. **Component 3:** Capacity building and knowledge management

### e) Needs of the recipient

Madagascar, a small island developing state (SIDS), is one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change impacts. The country's coastal ecosystems are highly susceptible to sea-level rise and extreme weather events. The project will support the Government of Madagascar in enhancing the resilience of coastal ecosystems and communities to climate change.

### f) Country oversight

The project is designed to capitalize on existing Malagasy policies and strategies, ensuring alignment with specific references to the Malagasy national development frameworks. It is aligned with the country's long-term vision for sustainable development and poverty reduction.

# Green Climate Fund

## Alignment with National Determined Contributions (NDCs)

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) aligns with the Intended Nationally Determined Contributions (INDCs), the National Adaptation Programs of Action (NAPAs), and the National Climate Change Policy. These documents provide a framework for countries to identify their needs, priorities, and strategies for adaptation and mitigation. The GCF supports countries in implementing these strategies by providing financial resources and technical assistance.

### 3.6. Efficiency and Effectiveness

**Scale: Medium to high**

- The co-financing rate of 1:1 is significant for an adaptation project of this nature against the standard investment. The proposed plan provides a benefit-cost ratio of 2.59 with an internal rate of return (IRR) of 30%.
- The proposal uses demonstrated cost-effectiveness based on a comparison of unit costs between the proposed project and similar projects in the region. The cost-effectiveness for the best practice measure (output 1) is estimated at USD 27,000 per hectare (USD 5 per tonne CO2 equivalent). This is comparable to other projects in the region.
- The proposal includes a comprehensive monitoring and evaluation plan that will allow for continuous assessment of project performance. There is also an emphasis on gender equality and social inclusion, which is critical to ensuring that benefits are distributed equitably.

  - Component 2: National Adaptation Plan (NAP).
  - Component 3: National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA) capacity building.
  - Component 4: Implementation of climate change adaptation measures.

# Greenpeace

## ESS: Community health, safety and security

Site communities will be impacted by the proposed project. The potential impacts include:

- Local employment opportunities.
- Increased economic activity.
- Improved local infrastructure.

These impacts are expected to be positive and long-lasting. The project will also provide direct and indirect benefits to the community through:

- Local procurement of goods and services.
- Local hiring of labour.
- Local investment in community projects.

## ESS: Land acquisition and resettlement

The ISM indicates that land acquisition is a physical development activity which will be limited to the site area. The project will not require large-scale land acquisitions as it does not involve:

- Large-scale land clearance.
- Large-scale land degradation.

Land will be acquired by the construction company through negotiation with landowners and local authorities. The resettlement process will be carried out in accordance with the principles of the UN Guiding Principles on Business and Human Rights. The project will also provide:

- Compensation for lost assets.
- Support for livelihood restoration.

## ESS: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The proposed project will have minimal impacts on biodiversity as it does not involve:

- Large-scale land clearing.
- Large-scale habitat destruction.

The project will be designed to avoid impacts on key species and habitats. The project will also contribute to the conservation of biodiversity by:

- Implementing best practice guidelines for biodiversity management.
- Monitoring and reporting on biodiversity impacts.

## KPI: Key Performance Indicators

The following indicators will be used to measure performance against the principles outlined in this ESS:

- Local employment rates.
- Local procurement rates.
- Community satisfaction with project impacts.

If these indicators are not met, the project may be required to take corrective action through restriction of activities or other measures.

# Green Impact Fund

## Implementation arrangements

The AI will have overall oversight to ensure that the UIPP is adequately followed. The Ministry of Energy and Energy Development (MEED) and the EIA Secretariat will be responsible for implementing this project. A Project Management Unit (PMU) will be established within MEED to oversee the implementation of the project. The PMU will work closely with the EIA Secretariat, the Ministry of Finance, and other relevant agencies.

Stakeholder engagement and grievance redress mechanisms are essential components of the UIPP. Stakeholders will be engaged through public consultations, community meetings, and other appropriate forums to ensure that their concerns and expectations are taken into account. The PMU will also establish a grievance redress mechanism to address any complaints or issues raised by project-affected communities.

### Gender policy

The AI provides a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the UIPP.

In this regard, the AI highlights the country's commitment to enhance gender equality and women's empowerment. The Ministry will ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with the provisions of the Project Affected Persons (PAP) Gender Policy. The Community Development Officer (CDO) will be responsible for ensuring that the PAP Gender Policy is properly implemented.

The gender assessment was informed by desk review and stakeholder consultations. The AI highlights the need to ensure equal opportunities for women and men in all aspects of the project. Women's participation in public life is also generally encouraged. Women are more likely to be targeted with a renewed understanding of the importance of gender equality, as well as the need to address issues related to gender-based violence.

The AI also recognizes that women and girls face significant barriers to accessing education and employment opportunities. The AI aims to promote equal access to education for all children, regardless of their gender or socio-economic status. The AI will also provide training and capacity-building programs to help women and girls gain the skills they need to participate in the labor force.

The AI will also work to ensure that women have equal access to decision-making processes and leadership opportunities. The AI will support initiatives that promote women's participation in public life, such as women's cooperatives and other community-based organizations.

**Page 108**

Fresh and nutritious food is a fundamental aspect of women’s health and well-being. Women work alongside men to cultivate crops and rear livestock for their families’ consumption. Gender roles in Madagascar reflect unique cultural attributes that influence women’s participation in agriculture and their access to resources. Women are responsible for planting, weeding, harvesting, processing, and marketing crops. They also manage household budgets and make decisions about food purchases and preparation. Women’s agricultural contributions are vital to the country’s economic development.

The Global Alliance for Improved Nutrition (GAIN) is committed to improving nutrition outcomes by working with a range of partners to address women’s nutritional needs. GAIN’s work in Madagascar focuses on empowering women and promoting gender equality through its programs. GAIN’s activities include addressing challenges identified in the greater action plan, such as food fortification, biofortification, and food systems. This also includes supporting women’s empowerment and joint social enterprises. GAIN’s work in Madagascar is part of a broader effort to promote sustainable development goals (SDGs) and improve nutrition outcomes for all.

GAIN’s activities include the following:

- **Food Fortification:** GAIN supports efforts to fortify staple foods with essential vitamins and minerals, which can help address micronutrient deficiencies.
- **Biofortification:** GAIN works with partners to develop and promote biofortified crops that naturally contain higher levels of nutrients.
- **Food Systems:** GAIN supports initiatives aimed at improving food systems and promoting sustainable agriculture.

GAIN’s work in Madagascar is part of a broader effort to promote sustainable development goals (SDGs) and improve nutrition outcomes for all. The organization works closely with local partners, including government agencies, non-governmental organizations, and private sector companies, to develop and implement programs that address the unique challenges facing women in Madagascar.

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall program assessment (medium risk)

- GAIN recognizes the need for a greater focus on gender equality in its programming. This is particularly important given the central role of women in agriculture and food security.
- GAIN’s work in Madagascar is dependent on continued funding from donors, which can be unpredictable.

#### 4.3.2 Gender equality (medium risk)

- An assessment of gender equality is necessary to ensure that the current program activities are inclusive and equitable for all participants.

# EU Green Deal Investment Plan

## I. Introduction

### a. Overview of the EU Green Deal Investment Plan (EIGIP)
The EU Green Deal Investment Plan is designed to support the financing of projects and activities that contribute to climate objectives. It aims to mobilize at least EUR 1 trillion in sustainable investments over the next decade.

### b. Role of the European Fund for Strategic Investments (EFSI)
The EFSI will be a key component of the EIGIP, focusing on removing regulatory barriers and providing financial support. The plan includes measures such as the extension of the Juncker Plan and the establishment of a European Green Deal Investment Fund.

## II. Project-Specific Evaluation Criteria

### a. Contribution to EU Goals
Projects must demonstrate how they contribute to at least one of the following:
- Climate objectives (e.g., reducing greenhouse gas emissions)
- Energy efficiency targets
- Renewable energy integration

### b. Financial Viability
The project's financial viability should be demonstrated through detailed business plans and financial projections. The plan should also outline how the project will achieve a reasonable return on investment.

### c. Environmental Sustainability
Projects must adhere to high environmental standards and comply with relevant regulations. They should also demonstrate how they contribute to sustainable development goals.

## III. Endorsement of EIGIP

### a. Process for Endorsement
The endorsement process involves an assessment by the European Commission, which includes:
- Review of project documents
- On-site visits
- Consultation with relevant stakeholders

### b. Economic and Social Impact
Projects must demonstrate significant economic and social benefits, including job creation and regional development.

## IV. Compliance with EU Regulations

### a. Regulatory Framework
The project must comply with all relevant EU regulations, including those related to state aid and environmental standards.

### b. Environmental and Social Standards (ESS)
Projects must adhere to the ESS, which include requirements for environmental protection and social responsibility.

### c. Reporting Requirements
Projects must submit regular reports on their progress and compliance with the approved policies and standards.

# Legal assessment

The Accreditation Minor Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 17 May 2018 ("AMA"), and became effective on 19 April 2019.

The Accreditation Board has not provided a legal opinion as to whether the AMA is binding. It has been understood that the AMA is not intended to be legally binding, although it does contain certain undertakings by the Accredited Entity (including the requirement to comply with the Accreditation Standards). The AMA also contains a clause providing that any flow-up agreement to be entered into between the parties will be governed by and construed in accordance with the laws of Singapore. GCP has based a material part of its assessment on previous and ongoing discussions with the Government of Singapore (the "Government") regarding the implementation of the AMA, including the Government's willingness to provide certain support to GCP in the event that the Accredited Entity is not able to perform its obligations under the AMA. The Government has also indicated that it would be prepared to enter into discussions with the Accredited Entity and GCP on the terms of a new agreement if the Accredited Entity is unable to perform its obligations under the AMA. The Government has further indicated that it would be willing to provide certain support to GCP in the event that the Accredited Entity is not able to perform its obligations under the AMA.

## List of proposed conditions (excluding legal)

In order for an agreement, it is recommended that any agreement by the Board to accredit an entity should include the following:

- Signature of the National Activity Agreement by a duly authorised signatory.
- Submission by the accredited entity to GCP of a certificate of registration, here and thereafter referred to as the "Certificate".
- Confirmation that the accredited entity has obtained all necessary approvals required by law for its activities.

The Certificate shall be signed by an authorised representative of the accredited entity. The Certificate shall also include a legal opinion confirming that the Accredited Entity has complied with all applicable laws and regulations.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP165

## Proposed name:
Building Climate Resilient Saline Islands in the Maldives

## Accepted entity:
Eigen International Cooperation Agency (EICA)

## Country(ies):
Republic of Maldives

## Project (proposal) no.:
Maldives

## L. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential
**Scale: Medium to high**

This is a medium-size funding proposal submitted by the Republic of Maldives which is a small island developing State (SIDS) located in the Indian Ocean (Bioswale). The final goal of the project is to enhance climate resilience and sustainable development for 2 years of salt-affected coastal areas. This grant contributes from the AF 34.1, per year a total funding amount of USD 500,000.

The Maldives has long coastline and low-lying land area. The population of the country is 787,600 with a population density of 1,299 people per km². The primary activities of the economy are tourism, fisheries, and shipping. The total area of the country is 300 km², and the length of coastline is 644 km.

The Maldives has a tropical monsoon climate with two distinct seasons: dry (or northeast monsoon) and wet (or southwest monsoon). The Maldivian economy is based on fishing and tourism. The building project focuses on the construction of artificial islands, which are designed to protect the existing infrastructure from flooding, saltwater intrusion, and coastal erosion.

The project will be financed by the EICA and other new funds. Moreover, island of Addu, Fuvahmulah, and Gan and Hithadhoo will be the main beneficiaries of this project.

All activities are managed by professionals in UNHCR, especially a well-trained team in the fielding of climate change adaptation projects. The project is expected to be completed within 2 years.

The building proposal is focused mainly on the establishment of the protection functions of natural sandy beaches and coral reefs as “natural infrastructure” and the establishment of a

# Disease Warning and Information Dissemination System (DWIDS)

The DWIDS strengthens the overall management capacity of populations at risk for diseases, hazards, and disasters with a focus on vulnerable groups. It aims to enhance the preparedness plans of local government units to reduce climate change adaptation challenges.

## Key Highlights

- **Early Warning Systems:** Enhanced by integrating new technologies.
- **Risk Communication:** Improved through the use of social media and other communication channels.
- **Community Engagement:** Increased to ensure the sustainability of project results.

### The DWIDS project components

The DWIDS project has two components:

1. **Component 1: DWIDS Implementation**
   - The component will be implemented in collaboration with the General Service Management (GSMI), the Department of Science and Technology (DOST), and the Philippine Atmospheric, Geophysical and Astronomical Services Administration (PAGASA).
   - It will focus on the development of early warning systems for climate change-related hazards such as floods, landslides, and storm surges.
   - The component will also provide training and capacity building to local government units.

2. **Component 2: DWIDS Implementation Support**
   - This component will be implemented at selected areas in collaboration with the Department of Environment and Natural Resources (DENR).
   - It aims to enhance the preparedness plans of local government units for climate change-related hazards.
   - The component will also provide training and capacity building to local government units.

### General Objective

The general objective of the DWIDS is to strengthen the overall management capacity of populations at risk for diseases, hazards, and disasters with a focus on vulnerable groups. It aims to enhance the preparedness plans of local government units to reduce climate change adaptation challenges.

### Specific Objectives

1. **Objective 1: Enhance Early Warning Systems**
   - Integrate new technologies into early warning systems.
   - Improve risk communication through social media and other channels.
   - Increase community engagement to ensure sustainability.

2. **Objective 2: Strengthen Preparedness Plans**
   - Develop preparedness plans for climate change-related hazards.
   - Provide training and capacity building to local government units.
   - Enhance the overall management capacity of populations at risk.

### Component 1: DWIDS Implementation

The component will be implemented in collaboration with GSMI, DOST, and PAGASA. It will focus on:

- Developing early warning systems for climate change-related hazards such as floods, landslides, and storm surges.
- Providing training and capacity building to local government units.

### Component 2: DWIDS Implementation Support

This component will be implemented at selected areas in collaboration with DENR. It aims to:

- Enhance the preparedness plans of local government units for climate change-related hazards.
- Provide training and capacity building to local government units.

### General Objective

The general objective of the DWIDS is to strengthen the overall management capacity of populations at risk for diseases, hazards, and disasters with a focus on vulnerable groups. It aims to enhance the preparedness plans of local government units to reduce climate change adaptation challenges.

# Sustainable development potential

## Climate action

### a)
The funding proposals contribute to achieving aspects of sustainable development and to participation in protecting the most vulnerable countries and populations against climate change. Activities planned in the funding proposal contribute to the implementation of the Paris Agreement on climate change, particularly Article 2.5, which aims to enhance the resilience and adaptive capacity of developing countries to climate-related hazards and natural disasters in a manner that fully respects human rights, the right to development, and international environmental law as stated in relevant General Assembly resolutions, including A/RES/70/1.

### b)
Activities planned in the funding proposal contribute to the climate action are strengthening resilience and adaptive capacity to climate-related hazards and natural disasters in developing countries. The activities will be implemented through the following SDGs: 13 (Climate Action), 9 (Industry, Innovation, and Infrastructure) and 11 (Sustainable Cities and Communities).

## Biodiversity conservation

### a)
According to SDG 14 around 600 million people live in low-lying coastal zones and this number is expected to increase due to climate change. Following SDG targets, target 14.2 which requires sustainable management and protection of marine and coastal ecosystems, target 14.3 which aims at minimizing the impacts of ocean acidification, including through enhanced scientific cooperation to improve understanding, and target 14.5 which aims to conserve at least 10 percent of coastal and marine areas, including through effective area-based conservation measures, are relevant. Activities planned in the funding proposal contribute to conserving biodiversity and the sustainable use of the oceans, seas and marine resources for sustainable development. The activities will be implemented through the following SDGs: 14 (Life Below Water) and 15 (Life on Land).

### b)
Activities planned in the funding proposal are aimed at conserving biodiversity and the sustainable use of the oceans, seas and marine resources for sustainable development. Activities will also contribute to achieving target 14.2 of SDG 14 which aims to sustainably manage and protect marine and coastal ecosystems.

## Gender

### a)
The Human Development Index (HDI) value for the Maldives is 0.698 for gender in equality, ranking 113 out of 187 countries. The Maldives has a high Gender Inequality Index value of 0.549, placing the country into a group of countries with high levels of inequality between women and men. The funding proposal aims to contribute to achieving SDG target 5.1 which seeks to end all forms of discrimination against all women and girls everywhere. Activities will be implemented through the following SDGs: 5 (Gender Equality) and 4 (Quality Education).

### b)
Activities planned in the funding proposal aim to achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls. The activities will be implemented through the following SDGs: 5 (Gender Equality), 10 (Reduced Inequalities) and 8 (Decent Work and Economic Growth).

The needs include the development of exposure adaptation measures, such as coastal land protection measures that are further based on vulnerability assessments.

# Greenpeace Report

## Sea Turtle Bycatch in Longline Fisheries

### Threats from the North
The small size of the islands and their low elevation make them susceptible to deforestation against severe weather events, as well as tsunamis.

### Nesting Beaches
Nesting beaches are located on the eastern coasts of the islands. The most important nesting beach is located in the northern part of the island, and about 0.8 to 1.6 meters on the central and southern slopes. The number of nests varies from year to year depending on the weather conditions.

### Sea Turtle Bycatch
Sea turtles are caught as bycatch in longline fisheries. The most common species caught are green turtles (_Chelonia mydas_) and hawksbill turtles (_Eretmochelys imbricata_). The number of turtles caught varies depending on the season, with the highest numbers caught during the nesting season.

### Historical Data
Historical data shows that sea turtle bycatch has been a problem in the region for many years. In the 1970s and 1980s, there were reports of large numbers of turtles being caught as bycatch in longline fisheries. The number of turtles caught has varied over the years, with some years seeing very high numbers and other years seeing much lower numbers.

### Offshore Waters
Offshore waters are the main areas where sea turtle bycatch occurs. Analysis of 60 years of data from 1958 to 2018 shows that the number of turtles caught has varied significantly over time, with some years seeing very high numbers and other years seeing much lower numbers.

### Beach Nourishment
Beach nourishment is a technique used to replenish sand on beaches. This can be done by pumping sand from offshore areas onto the beach. Beach nourishment can help to protect nesting beaches from erosion and can also provide additional habitat for sea turtles.

### Sea Surface Temperatures (SST)
Sea surface temperatures (SST) are an important factor in determining the distribution of sea turtles. SST can affect the migration patterns of sea turtles, as well as their feeding habits and reproductive cycles. Changes in SST can have significant impacts on the populations of sea turtles.

### Historical Data
Historical data shows that sea turtle bycatch has been a problem in the region for many years. In the 1970s and 1980s, there were reports of large numbers of turtles being caught as bycatch in longline fisheries. The number of turtles caught has varied over the years, with some years seeing very high numbers and other years seeing much lower numbers.

### Offshore Waters
Offshore waters are the main areas where sea turtle bycatch occurs. Analysis of 60 years of data from 1958 to 2018 shows that the number of turtles caught has varied significantly over time, with some years seeing very high numbers and other years seeing much lower numbers.

### Beach Nourishment
Beach nourishment is a technique used to replenish sand on beaches. This can be done by pumping sand from offshore areas onto the beach. Beach nourishment can help to protect nesting beaches from erosion and can also provide additional habitat for sea turtles.

countries are strongly encouraged to develop a nationally determined contribution (NDC) report that he

nds to achieve the long-term goals of the Paris Agreement. The NDC report should be consistent with the

principles, including transparency, fairness, respecting the principle of common but differentiated responsibilities and

respective capabilities, promoting gender-based decision-making, a cultural perspective on climate change, and

respect for human rights, the rights of indigenous peoples and local communities, intergenerational equity, and the

protection of sub-national, national, regional, and global ecosystems and biodiversity. The NDC report should also

reflect the principles of the Paris Agreement, including the principle of common but differentiated responsibilities

and respective capabilities, as well as the provisions of the Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD), the United

Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and the Convention on International Trade in Endan

gered Species (CITES). The NDC report should also reflect the principles of the Rio Declaration on Environment and

Development, including the precautionary approach to human health and the environment, intergenerational equity,

and the principle that the needs of future generations must be taken into account.

The revised Second National Communication (SNC) submitted in 2019 highlights the country's commitment to

achieving climate change mitigation and adaptation goals. The SNC also provides an overview of the country's

climate change challenges, as well as its vision, objectives, and strategies for addressing these challenges. The SNC

also includes information on the country's greenhouse gas emissions, as well as its efforts to reduce these emissions.

The SNC also highlights the country's commitment to achieving the goals of the Paris Agreement, including the goal

of limiting global temperature increase to 1.5 degrees Celsius above pre-industrial levels by 2030 and achieving

net-zero emissions by 2050. The SNC also includes information on the country's efforts to adapt to the impacts of

climate change, as well as its plans for future adaptation efforts.

The climate change policy framework (2015-2025) outlines crucial steps towards addressing climate change in the

country. The framework emphasizes the importance of the SNC and the Maldives' determination to achieve high

ambition goals. The framework also highlights the country's commitment to achieving the goals of the Paris

Agreement, including the goal of limiting global temperature increase to 1.5 degrees Celsius above pre-industrial

levels by 2030 and achieving net-zero emissions by 2050.

The national adaptation programmes of action (NAPAs) of Maldives were developed in response to the urgent need

to address the adverse effects of climate change on the country's development. The NAPA was developed with the

support of the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and the Global Environment Facility (GEF). The NAPA

aims to identify and prioritize adaptation measures that are most relevant to the Maldives' specific context, including

the country's vulnerability to climate change. The NAPA also aims to provide a framework for implementing these

measures, as well as monitoring and evaluating their effectiveness.

# The GCF readiness programmes

The GCF readiness programmes also confirm crucial knowledge management in the first priority for adaptation. According to the readiness programme, about 30 per cent of the countries that have received funding from the GCF are implementing projects and programmes related to climate change adaptation. Since the GCF at Marrakech this year highlights that lack of planning and implementation capacities is a major constraint to effective adaptation, the GCF readiness programme has been designed to address these challenges. The GCF readiness programme aims to build capacity in developing countries for the development of robust National Adaptation Plans (NAPs) and Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs). It also provides support to enhance the integration of climate change into national policies, strategies, plans and budgets.

## 1.1

One of the first recurring activities (EoI) of the funding proposal is the development of a readiness programme that will be implemented by the GCF in collaboration with other partners. The readiness programme aims to support developing countries in strengthening their capacities for planning, implementing and monitoring climate change adaptation projects. The readiness programme is also designed to enhance the capacity of developing countries to access GCF resources through direct access entities (DAEs). The readiness programme will be implemented by the GCF in collaboration with other partners.

# Relates to the feasibility of off measures for beach nourishment which is considered as the preferred process as a first measure for the paradigm shift process.

## Implementation of the ICZM plan

The implementation of the ICZM plan will be carried out in two phases:
- **Phase I**: Development of the planning proposal, including the effects being caused by the measures and the expected benefits.
- **Phase II**: Long-term maintenance of the beach nourishment measures with a high standard of quality.

### Long-term maintenance

The long-term maintenance of the beach nourishment measures will be carried out in accordance with the following principles:
1. **Long-term maintenance** is considered as an integral part of the planning process and should be included in the planning proposal.
2. **Long-term maintenance** is a prerequisite for the sustainability of the beach nourishment measures.

### Development of the planning proposal

The development of the planning proposal will include the following steps:
1. **Identification of the effects** caused by the measures, including the expected benefits and costs.
2. **Development of the planning proposal**, including the expected benefits and costs.
3. **Long-term maintenance** is considered as an integral part of the planning process.

### Long-term maintenance

The long-term maintenance of the beach nourishment measures will be carried out in accordance with the following principles:
1. **Long-term maintenance** is considered as an integral part of the planning process.
2. **Long-term maintenance** is a prerequisite for the sustainability of the beach nourishment measures.

## Collaboration with stakeholders

Collaboration with the private sector's beach maintenance and the large government-driven dredging companies will be essential to ensure that the beach nourishment measures are carried out in accordance with the principles outlined above. The success of the beach nourishment measures will depend on the collaboration between the public and private sectors.

## Funding proposal

The funding proposal for the implementation of the ICZM plan will include the following elements:
1. **Funding** for the development of the planning proposal.
2. **Funding** for the long-term maintenance of the beach nourishment measures.
3. **Funding** for the collaboration with the private sector's beach maintenance and the large government-driven dredging companies.

The funding proposal will be developed in accordance with the principles outlined above and will include a detailed budget for each phase of the implementation process.

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment

## Green Climate Fund Funding Protocol v2.0

### Introduction

Gender assessment data provide an overview of the gender situation in the Maldives, with a specific focus on providing and improving the inclusion of gender in climate change projects. The Maldives is one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change impacts, particularly sea-level rise, coastal erosion, and salinization. These issues disproportionately affect women due to their roles as primary caregivers and their dependence on natural resources for livelihoods.

Gender inequalities are deeply entrenched in the Maldives' social structure, including gender-based violence, unequal access to education and employment opportunities, and limited participation in decision-making processes. Data gathering is essential to understand these issues better and to develop effective interventions.

The Maldives has made significant progress towards achieving the Millennium Development Plan 2006-2015 and the Millennium Development Goals. The country has also developed a National Gender Policy and a National Action Plan on Gender Equality, which aim to promote gender equality and empower women.

### Strengthening and Scaling Up International Obligations and Assessed Learned Lessons

The Maldives is committed to strengthening its international obligations and scaling up lessons learned from national interventions. The country has made significant progress in promoting gender equality and empowering women. The government has also developed a National Gender Policy and a National Action Plan on Gender Equality, which aim to promote gender equality and empower women.

### Resilience of Communities in Vulnerable Situations in the Atolls and Learny Islands and Their Response to the Increasing Threats of Rising Sea Levels and Coastal Erosion

The Maldives is highly vulnerable to climate change. The country's low-lying atolls are particularly susceptible to sea-level rise, coastal erosion, and salinization. These issues disproportionately affect women due to their roles as primary caregivers and their dependence on natural resources for livelihoods.

The Maldives' vulnerability to climate change and extreme weather events remains a significant challenge. The country's commitment to sustainable development is under threat from the increasing frequency of extreme weather events, which are exacerbated by climate change. The government has developed a National Adaptation Plan and a National Action Plan on Gender Equality, which aim to promote gender equality and empower women.

The Maldives is highly vulnerable to natural disasters, with the exception of the Indian Ocean tsunami in 2004. The country has also been affected by droughts, floods, and cyclones. These events have had significant impacts on the economy, particularly in the tourism industry, which accounts for approximately 75% of the country's GDP. The Maldives is also highly vulnerable to climate change, with sea-level rise expected to affect the country's low-lying atolls.

The Maldives has experienced rapid development in recent years, largely as a result of natural disasters and has developed a National Adaptation Plan and a National Action Plan on Gender Equality, which aim to promote gender equality and empower women.

Apart from that, the fisheries sector employs around 25,000 people, which is also one of the major sources of income for the country. The agriculture sector employs around 3,000 people.

Environmental degradation, air pollution, and ranges of the natural disaster would be increased due to the climate change and global warming. The vulnerability index of the country is very high and it is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world. ## 4. The Gender Context in the Maldives

Concerned about the lack of South Asia Maldivian women face discrimination in basic aspects of life such as employment, education and health care. The country exhibits a classic case of a relatively prosperous country where gender inequalities in basic well-being are still evident. Women and girls suffer from unequal access to education, health services, and decision-making roles within the family. They also face discrimination in the labour market and are under-represented in political life.

The government has taken steps to address these issues by implementing policies that promote gender equality and women's empowerment. These include measures such as providing equal pay for equal work, increasing the number of women in leadership positions, and promoting women's participation in decision-making processes.

## 4.1 Gender Inequality Index

Gender inequality is measured using the Gender Inequality Index (GII). The United Nations Development Programme uses the GII and Gender Development Index to measure gender equality around the world.

The GII presents a composite measure of gender inequality using three dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment, and the labour market. The GII can be interpreted as the loss in human development due to gender inequality. The Maldives had a GII of 0.369 in 2018 and ranks 87 out of 162 countries assessed.

- United Nations Development Programme. (2016). Human Development Report 2016: Human Development for Everyone. New York: UNDP.

## Table 4.1 Gender Inequality Index for Malawi between 2015 and 2019

The Gender Development Index (GDI) is based on the sex-disaggregated Human Development Index (HDI), which itself is a ratio of women to men HDI. The GDI classifies countries into four categories: very low, low, medium, and high human development. The Gender Empowerment Measure (GEM) assesses women's participation in power structures, their access to economic resources, and their ability to influence major decisions that affect their lives. It includes years of schooling for children and income for adults aged 25 and older, and the 2018 Human Development Report for Malawi is a GDI of 0.697 or a GEM of 0.344.

| Gender Inequality Index (GII)| 0.434  | 0.424  | 0.415  | 0.406  |
| Human Development Index (HDI) | 0.597  | 0.587  | 0.578  | 0.569  |

The Global Gender Gap Index (GGI) of the World Economic Forum's Global Gender Gap Report measures gender-based disparities in four areas: economic participation and opportunity, educational attainment, health and survival, and political empowerment. The GGI score ranges from 0 to 1, where 0 indicates inequality and 1 indicates equality. The Malawi GDI was based on GDI is 0.694 in 2018 and the HDI is 0.578.

| Category                     | 2015   | 2016   | 2017   | 2018   |
|------------------------------|--------|--------|--------|--------|
| Economic participation and opportunity | 0.943  | 0.934  | 0.925  | 0.916  |
| Educational attainment      | 0.789  | 0.779  | 0.769  | 0.759  |
| Health and survival         | 0.943  | 0.934  | 0.925  | 0.916  |
| Political empowerment        | 0.843  | 0.834  | 0.825  | 0.816  |

Source: UNDP 2019 Human Development Report.

## 4.2 Poverty

The proportion of the population living below the national poverty line of MWK 2,720 per day increased from 35% in 2015 to 37% in 2018.
Unemployment rates have been high for many years and are estimated at around 20%. The Gender Inequality Index (GII) for Malawi is 0.434, with a GDI of 0.694.

Source: UNDP 2019 Human Development Report.

# Gender Assessment

## Green Climate Fund Funding Protocols v3.1

Even though unemplotment decreased among females, the unemployment rate was still highest for females, especially for females in rural areas and between 20 and 64 years old.

### Health

The strategic framework for the government's National Reproductive Health Strategy for 2014-2023 aims to improve maternal health. Significant progress has been made in promoting maternal and child health services. The percentage of deliveries attended by skilled birth attendants increased from 59% in 2008 to 76% in 2013, with the majority of these deliveries occurring in health facilities. The number of antenatal care visits also increased from 49% in 2008 to 73% in 2013. Maternal mortality has decreased from 59 per 100,000 births in 2008 to 33 per 100,000 births in 2013.

### Education

The evidence is also among the few developing countries in the world that have fully achieved all six of the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) for education. The government's Education Master Plan 2007-2016 has been successful in achieving universal primary education and gender equality in education. The literacy rate among females aged between 15 and 24 years is over 98%. The percentage of females attending secondary school was around 38% in 2008, increasing to 47% in 2013.

### Political Representation and Decision Making

Household decision making appears to be relatively gender egalitarian in the Maldives. A national survey (DHS) found that the husband and wife make joint household decisions equally. However, women are less likely than men to participate in political discussions or public meetings. Women's participation in political life is low, but major household purchases are decided jointly by men and women.

### References

- ADB 2015: Maldives - Gender Equality Diagnostic in Selected Sectors
- EIJNEN, Joris, and Bina J. Williams (eds). 2014. Understanding Development in the Maldives. The Hague: The Netherlands Institute of International Relations Clingendael.
- UNDP 2013: Human Development Report 2013 - The Rise of South Asia

### 4.6 Labor Force

The 2008 Employment Act stipulates the provisions for equal access to employment by men and women, prohibits the dismissal of a woman on marriage or maternity leave and grants the dismissal from any job on grounds of pregnancy. The Act also provides for equal pay for work of equal value, regardless of gender, and prohibits discrimination based on sex, marital status, disability, age, religion, political opinion, nationality, professional and family responsibility, the protection of special needs of pregnant women and women's representation on corporate boards.

### 4.7 Access to Resources

Maldivian women have average in owning and control of household assets in Maldives families. Women own 10% of land and 5% of boats. This statistic is in part due to gender-differentiated property rights.

## World Economic Forum 2014, 2018, 2019, 2020

The Global Gender Gap Report 2014, 2018 and 2019 shows that the Maldives – Gender Equality Index has declined over the years. The World Economic Forum's Global Gender Gap Index 2019 ranks the Maldives at 103 out of 153 countries surveyed.

# Annex III – Gender Assessment

## Green Climate Fund Funding Protocols v3.5

### 4.8 Gender-based Violence

According to a study by UN Women (2017), domestic violence against women is very high in Madagascar and has increased over the past ten years. The study found that 69% of women have experienced physical and/or sexual violence from their intimate partners, with 35% experiencing it within the last year. This violence is often linked to alcohol consumption by the perpetrator.

Including the Gender Equality Act (2001), Domestic Violence Prevention Act (2014), Sexual Offences Act (2014), and Anti-Sexual Harassment Act (2015) were enacted to protect women from gender-based violence. However, these laws are not always enforced due to a lack of awareness among the population.

### 5.1 Right to Equality and Assessment Framework Indicators

#### 5.1.1 International Gender Framework

- **International Commitments to Gender Equality**

The Framework is based on a variety of international conventions and agreements that address gender equality, including:

- **Universal Declaration of Human Rights (UDHR)**
- **Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW)**
- **International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights (ICCPR)**
- **International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights (ICESCR)**

The Framework is also aligned with the Commonwealth Action Plan for Gender Equality and regional commitments such as the SADC Gender Protocol and the Maputo Protocol.

- **The Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) and Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)**

The MDGs included gender equality targets, and the SDGs have expanded on these goals. The MDG target 3 was to reduce maternal mortality by three-quarters between 1990 and 2015, and MDG 5 focused on improving maternal health. The SDGs include a specific goal for gender equality (SDG 5).

- **The Paris Agreement**

The Paris Agreement includes provisions related to gender equality and climate change, emphasizing the need to address both issues simultaneously.

### References

1. UN Women (2017). Study on Domestic Violence against Women in Madagascar.
2. Gender Equality Act (2001).
3. Domestic Violence Prevention Act (2014).
4. Sexual Offences Act (2014).
5. Anti-Sexual Harassment Act (2015).

# Annex III – Gender Assessment

## Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal v3.1

### (i) Promote Gender Equality and Empower Women as not achieved

#### (ii) Enhance Gender Equality and Empowerment of Women and Girls through MDG Goal 3

##### (a) Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)

A new global development agenda known as the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development was adopted by the United Nations General Assembly in September 2015. The 2030 Agenda includes 17 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), which were developed to succeed the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs). The SDGs aim to promote prosperity and economic opportunity, strengthen universal peace and international cooperation, foster resilient infrastructure, encourage inclusive economic growth, build effective institutions, and tackle climate change and environmental challenges.

The Ministry of Environment and Energy (MoEE) is a key player in the implementation of the SDGs. The MoEE has been actively involved in the development and implementation of the SDGs since their inception. The ministry has worked closely with other government agencies to ensure that the SDGs are integrated into national policies and strategies.

The 2030 Agenda includes intersectionality of the SDGs and the indivisibility of peace, security, human rights, education, health, gender equality, water and sanitation, energy, cities, oceans, and ecosystems. The MoEE has been actively involved in the implementation of these goals and has worked to ensure that they are integrated into national policies and strategies.

The 2030 Agenda also emphasizes the importance of gender equality and women's empowerment. The MoEE has been working to promote gender equality and empower women through various initiatives, such as providing access to education and health care, promoting women's participation in decision-making processes, and supporting women's economic empowerment.

### 1. Government Structures to Address Gender Issues

The government structures to address gender issues have shifted multiple times over the last 25 years. The Ministry of Gender was established in 1993. However, since then, the name of the Gender Ministry has changed several times. In 2014, it was established as the Ministry of Women and Children's Affairs (MoWCA). This ministry was later merged with the Ministry of Social Welfare, Gender and Community Development to form the Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Welfare in November 2018.

The government structures for gender issues are as follows:

- To improve the lives of children, women, elderly and persons with disabilities with programs and services that benefit the entire population.
- To promote the rights of women and children from all forms of abuse and violence.
- To provide support to vulnerable groups in society.
- To ensure the rights of women and children are respected and protected.

All persons are guaranteed the same rights and freedoms under the Constitution of the Republic of Madagascar (2010).

### 2. National Gender Policies

The government has developed various policies to promote gender equality and empower women, including:

- **National Policy on Gender Equality**: This policy aims to promote gender equality and empower women in all aspects of life.
- **National Action Plan for the Implementation of the Beijing Platform for Action (1995)**: This plan outlines the steps that the government will take to implement the Beijing Platform for Action, which is a global strategy for promoting gender equality and empowering women.

### 3. Gender Mainstreaming

The government has been working to mainstream gender into all sectors of society, including:

- **Gender Equality in Education**: The government has been working to promote gender equality in education by providing equal access to education for both boys and girls.
- **Gender Equality in Health**: The government has been working to promote gender equality in health by providing equal access to health care for both men and women.

### 4. Gender Budgeting

The government has been working to integrate gender into budgeting processes, including:

- **Gender Responsive Budgeting**: The government has been working to ensure that budgets are designed to promote gender equality and empower women.

# Gender Assessment

## Economic Empowerment of Women, Girls and Enforcement, Special Needs and Concerns of Vulnerable Groups

Economic empowerment of women, girls and enforcement, special needs and concerns of vulnerable groups is a key component of gender equality. It includes equal access to sustainable opportunities, harnessing of women and girls' potential in the economy, and ensuring that women's rights are protected. Women's economic empowerment is essential for making and implementing decisions and policies that affect their lives.

### Equality Policy

The National Gender Equality Policy and Framework for Action (Draft) constitutes President Mahama's vision to promote gender equality in Ghana. It was launched on 8th March, 2015 by the Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Protection. The policy is an outcome of a consultative process that involved key stakeholders including government ministries, departments and agencies, civil society organizations, development partners and other relevant institutions.

The National Gender Equality Policy (Draft) includes the vision, guiding principles, policy goals, objectives and strategies for promoting gender equality in Ghana. It also outlines the roles and responsibilities of various stakeholders in achieving gender equality.

### Guiding Principles

- Guiding principle 1: Women's empowerment is a key to national development.
- Guiding principle 2: Gender equality is a human right and an essential element of democracy.
- Guiding principle 3: Women and girls are equal participants in the development process.
- Guiding principle 4: Women and girls have the right to live free from discrimination, violence and exploitation.

### Policy Goals

- Goal 1: The promotion of gender equality is a national priority.
- Goal 2: The empowerment of women and girls is essential for sustainable development.
- Goal 3: The elimination of all forms of discrimination against women and girls.
- Goal 4: The promotion of women's economic empowerment and participation in the economy.

### Gender Equality Act

Gender Equality Act was enacted on 12 August 2016. The Act delineates provisions of general application, special measures for the advancement of women and the establishment of a National Gender Machinery to ensure effective implementation of the Act.

# Annex III – Gender Assessment

## Green Climate Fund Funding Priorities v3.0

### Gender in Policies and the Duties and Responsibilities of the State Institutions and Other Relevant Actors

There are no specific duties:

1. To ensure that women enjoy equal rights, without discrimination based on sex or gender; the right to equal remuneration for work of equal value; non-discrimination; and equality of opportunity and treatment in employment and occupation.
2. To ensure that women have access to justice and effective remedies for gender-based violence.
3. To ensure that women enjoy equal terms with men, human rights fundamental rights, and freedoms in political, economic, social, cultural and other fields.
4. In accordance with the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW).
5. To eliminate discrimination against women in all matters relating to marriage and family relations.
6. To establish gender equality principles in state institutions, businesses, civil society organizations, and other relevant actors.
7. To ensure that all forms of violence against women and girls are eliminated.

### Deconcentration Act (2019)

The Deconcentration Act (7/2019) was enacted on 2019. The Act and its subsequent amendments have been implemented to decentralize the powers and responsibilities of the Government to the District Councils, Island Councils, and Island Councils, in which one third are women.

In addition, the Women’s Development Committee (WDC) of each island established under the Act will be integrated into a process that makes possible the active participation of women in decision-making processes. This will ensure that women have equal opportunities to participate in the formulation of budget regulations.

### Gender Issues in Response to the Projected Climate Induced Coastal Flooding Risks in Atolls and Lands

The gender issues are addressed by women who are recording the adverse impacts of climate change on their livelihoods, including the effects of sea-level rise. The women’s groups have been actively involved in public consultation meetings, where recommendations are provided. This follows and addresses the need to ensure that gender equality is considered in all aspects of climate change adaptation planning and implementation. The issues raised by the women highlight the unique security threats that affect women as a result of climate change.

- (Female) Quoted: In the cases of a disaster, which can be as much as 10 years, women are often left with no support. The government needs to ensure that they have enough resources to manage the aftermath of a natural disaster.

### Issues related to Component 3

- The evaluation plans are not planned, or even planned, they are not well disseminated to the communities.
- There is no clear plan for how to manage overdue loans in case of disaster. This affects both men and women who would decide how to manage overdue loans in case of disaster.

# Issues related to Component 1

## There are no specific issues raised during the consultation meetings for the activities under Component 1

### (3) Conclusions drawn from the results of the qualitative assessments to the Project

Participation of women and their subordinate groups into decision making is essential for the project. The participation of women in the decision-making process should be examined, e.g., equal numbers between men and women.

Most of participants are conduct both cleaning and farming activities. They are also involved in community meetings and other social activities. The majority of the population is Muslim and they are practicing Islam. The majority of the population is also practicing Christianity. As 10% of the population are the Matriarchs and Muslims, the spiritual attention to the Muslims are be considered during the project implementation. The majority of the population are also practicing Christianity.

The WOCAs also provide another entry point to take women's concerns on board, and their role in the community is important. The WOCAs are one of the mandates of MUFISS. The Project will need to coordinate with MUFISS on the implementation of the WOCAs.

### 7. Recommendations

#### (1) Necessary activities for mainstreaming gender into the Project activities:

- Gender equality should be integrated in all project activities.
- Gender equality should be integrated in all project activities.
- Gender equality should be integrated in all project activities.
- Gender equality should be integrated in all project activities.

#### (2) Activities to ensure gender equality and equal opportunities for women and men:

- Conduct of community-based consultations ensuring that all segments of the population, including women, are represented in the consultation process. The consultation stage is essential in the Baseline Information Plan (BIP) and Gender Action Plan.
- Setting of gender rules in the lending process to ensure gender equality and equal opportunities for women and men.
- Establishment of gender mechanisms, accessible to both women and men, to be able to address issues related to gender equality.
- Development and implementation of gender training programs on information sharing and communication skills.

#### (3) Activities to promote women's participation in public life:

- Gender equality should be integrated in all project activities.
- Gender equality should be integrated in all project activities.
- Gender equality should be integrated in all project activities.

# Annex VIII - Gender Assessment

## Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal v2.1

- Institution of national gender-based institutions and organizations as main stakeholders of GCF.
- Development and revision of SEP, as well as gender assessment and GAP, and ensuring gender mainstreaming in the project cycle.
- Encouragement of women's participation in decision-making processes at all levels, including local communities, and their empowerment to become agents of change.
- Establishment of a gender focal point within the GCF Secretariat, and provision of adequate resources for gender-related activities and budget.
- Development of guidelines on gender equality and social inclusion.
- Integration of gender equality into project implementation, including monitoring activities for the GCF, especially in relation to gender equality indicators that can be easily assessed with existing tools.
- Report: Initial Gender Equality Report and Final Gender Equality Report.

## Project objectives related to gender equality

These will include but not be limited to:

1. Ensuring equal access to resources and opportunities for women and men.
2. Addressing gender-based violence, including sexual harassment, and ensuring that the project objectives are met in a safe environment.
3. Addressing gender differences within the project design and implementation, this includes:
   - Ensuring that the project is designed and implemented by both men and women.
   - Ensuring that the project is designed and implemented by both men and women.
4. Ensuring that the project is designed and implemented by both men and women.

## Recommendations

To conduct thorough assessments in the project area, gender mainstreaming should be integrated into the project cycle. This includes:

1. Integrating gender equality into project design and implementation.
2. Ensuring equal access to resources and opportunities for women and men.
3. Addressing gender-based violence, including sexual harassment.

## Monitoring and Evaluation

During project implementation, qualitative assessments will be conducted on the gender-specific performance indicators. Monitoring tools such as the Gender Equality Index (GEI) will be used to measure progress in gender equality. The GEI will be administered by a project team member who is trained in gender equality issues.
